VERY AWKWARD, INDEED.—The most awkward man in the world, without doubt, lives in Ten- nessee. He recently shot a dog, and in explaining the accident to the dog's owner shot him. Later in showing how the tragedy occurred, he shot the coroner. He has been liberated now for fear he will try to explain it to somebody else.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DPIAR NET.Ltz, A charming hat is to be seen with a blue frieze costume. It is composed of pastel-blue panne, and it has an upturned brim lined with guipure, and bordered with narrow fur. This brim is laced through with blue ribbon, tied in a cluster of loops on the left side towards the back. A very pale pink velvet and silk rose with its foliage is placed near the top of the crown on the left side. Another pretty hat of white beaver shows broad bands of black velvet on the crown and upturned brim, and choux of cream lace, with small rosettes of pale pink quilted chiffon. The newest veil simulates a spider's web. It has no spots upon it. Long lace veils are coming in. Many are tied in a bow, with ends falling over the hair in the centre of back, as the ends are crossed on the back of the hat, and breu;zht under the chin, whore they are tied in a bow.-

A few of the winter jackets are more like coats with long tails, affecting the style of a man's coat, and when worn by a fashionable and tall woman who can afford plenty of clothes in change they have a certain style, but these fancies are not for the woman of small and limited dress allowance, who must exerise a greater care, and never get any- thing too markedly a fashion of the hour. These coats are very simple in form, and quietly trimmed with an edging of velvet on the cross or plain mohair braid. Collars are often edged with gold thread, and even a triple collar is sometimes seen. A stylish dress for skating or cold drives was of sealskin fur. The bolero ended in a small rounded point behind, after passing underneath a wide waistband of black satin. Very smart looking is black panne, marbled so as to form a close imita- tion of broadtail ; it makes up into charming winter gowns. Nearly all gowns are of plush or of soft hairy materials, homespun; sable cloths, etc., and the supremacy of velvet and Dann° is un- rivalled.

=art women continue to wear elegant gowns of cloth, in the most delicate tones of colour:ng, without giving a single thought to the gowns being ruined through their getting wet or being splashed with mud. If the dress finances are good it matters not, but if they are limited, and few changes of dress are at hand, then it is infinitely wiser to select some sensible coloured cloth, especially if the gown is intended for crdinary wear. Gowns of pale shrimp-pink or delicate blues or mauves are certainly more in keeping with dry, spring weather. A serviceable costume may be made from almost any description of black, brown, or grey material, and smartness imparted by the introduction of some bright colouring in the form of a blouse, yoke, vest, waist-belt, collar, tie, petticoat or dress linings. The same touch of colour can well be introduced into the hat or toque. This being Coronation year, red is to be freely used as a trimming for black dresses. Though so vivid a colouring, it lends itself admirably to black, greatly relieving the monotony of that sombre material. It is made the keynote of many black costumes. For instance, one model is com- posed of black poplin, with red silk linings. The fitting skirt is accompanied by a tucked red silk blouse and black bolero. The waistbelt is of black velvet, and so is the cravat, also collarband, while the petticoat is made nf red silk, with frills at the hem.

Perhaps Im, y venture to iV the following dress hints on acooant ~f their ',l,(4u:ncoss scarfs in Persian patterns are vkzy serviceable to throw over one's shoulders when wearing a deco!. lete evening gown. Those of white mull or mousseline are appliqued, hand painted, and embroidered in heavy silks. They also make delightful flans and boleros to wear with thin evening gowns. Velvet ribbon is being used in great quantities. It is made up into knots, bows, and huge rosettes; it is appliqued flat so as to form stripes, plaid effects, and lozenges. When different widths or ribbors are worked together, the effect is as com- plicated as Scotch plaid. For women who have passed their first youth and those whose figures present a certain amount of embonpoint the three- quarter length coat and the Eton with the postilion back are eminently suitable styles. It is very easy to lengthen a skirt which has grown too short by letting it down with a lace yoke or put- ting a wide lace insertion between the ruffles and the body of the skirt. - -

THE EXAMINER 1411111 PLY JANUARY 31 190 j

WOMEN'S CHAT. 6EOI The King likes to be brief in all things, and may therefore be expected to set the fashion for making a dinner a less protracted and luxurious iffair,than it has become in Society circles. It is not that the King is unappreciative of good things, and His Majesty not in- frequently manifests his interest in the menu, and orders its details for the day. But except for great and ceremonious occasions, the King does not care to make dinner a pretentions and fatiguing function, and the meal cannot be too short or simple for the Queen Con- sort. The Queen always eats but very little, but then it is not always those with good appetites that enjoy the best bealth,as indeed most long-livers are those who have been small eaters. The visit bf the Prince of Wales to Germany is not one of the essential events of the Coronation year, but it serves nevertheless to remind us that in the course of the next few months a great deal of the time of the Royal Family will be occupied by the State and public functions, which may possibly prove to be rather a severe strain upon their physical resources. But we remember that Queen Victoria, notwithstanding her years, was not accustomed to spare herself when it was a question of promoting the welfare of her subjects, and there is every reason to believe that the King and Queen will follow in the steps of that beloved lady, the anniversary of whose death was but recentl observed. Among the voluntary engagements of the King and Queen, is one which is exciting great interest at ' Plymouth and Deronport, their Majesties having promised to be present at the launch of the fine new battleship " Queen " early in March. All the Royal Palaces have teen in the hands of the decorators being thoroughly done up, and Princess Henry of Battenberg will shortly be able to occupy her suite of rooms, in Kensington Palace, which have now become ber town residence. For the past year Princess Henry has been living quietly abroad, or in the Isle of Wight, the death of her late Majesty the Queen, bring- ing greater changes into be life than to any other mem- ber of the Royal Family. The Princess will to some I extent now give up her seclusion, and in a year or two ' she will be recopied with the " coming out" ot her daughter. The Royal Prircesses who will make their entry into society this season, include Princess Alice of Albany, Princess Beatrice of Saxe-Coburg, and possibly Princess Patricia of Connaught, who will be sixteen in March. Princess Ens of Battenberg who is a year younger than her elder brother, will be fifteen next October.

At all the fashionable weddings long trains of bridee- maids are the rule. Miss Heligar bad nine, Lady Annabel Crewe-Miloes bad twelve, and Lady Helen Stuart ten. At Lady Helen's wedding the first four were little girls in Empire frocks, and the other six wore gowr s of white" quicksilver" silk,with white felt hats. One of these was Lady Viola Talbot, a niece of the Marchioness of Londonderry,who made her first appear- ance in society on this occasion. She will be presented at one of the early courts, and will be much with Lady Londonderry in future, as Lady Helen, now the wife of Lord Stavordale is the only daughter of the Marquis and Marchioness of Londonderry. ' Lady Helen Stavordale is a great favourite in Society, ' and received an immense number of wedding presents. These came not only from personal friends tied relatives, but also from several public bodies and tenants, and there were not a few from theatrical people,Lady Helen being an accomplished amateur actress. Altogether the presents exceeded seven hundred in number, and they were said to amount to no less than between three and four hundred thousand pounds in value. Some of the most imprrtant, however, came very near to being lost. Everyone knows that an expectant bridegroom is apt to become forgetful, and Lord Stavordale, in taking his gifts to the brideseraids, to Londonderry House, left the box of valuable jewels in the cab. Before the cabman knew the contents of the parcel, he received another call, and hastily put the package into the horse's nose- bag, where it remained unheeded till the next morning. The precious gems were then restored through the police, and no doubt the cabman found that it was the best night's work he had ever done in his life. The Princess of Wales has a great dislike for black in which she never looks her best, and Her Royal High. ness will greatly welcome the change into coloured dresses. It seems to have been thought that colours would be permissible at Court at the end of the long period of outward mourning for our late Queen, but this was not so on account of the death of the Empress Frederick last August. The change has not therefore been so sudden as some people expected, though the Princesses, it is true, are now appearing in half mourn- ing colours. Several membere of the Royal Family have also been seen at the theatre, and the Princess of Wales, who is especially fond of a good play, has lately been to two or three morning performances, Everyone knows how fond the Ring was of going to the theatre, when Prince of Wales, but probably His Majesty's visits to the playhouse will not be so frequent now. Those women ordering a new walking costume i which to face the bright, searching spring sunshinen will do well to select a Louis XV, jacket, or the bolero , and a perfectly plain skirt, cut to flow out moderately at the feet—not a flounce—and to just clear the ground. The bolero seems to be in greater tavour than ever, and is smarter looking even than the Louis Xv coat, but in view of the wintry weather, the wise ones say is to fall to our lot, the coat is perhaps the wiser investment. Many of the newest biomes to wear under the bolero or coat, are cut away at the neck, where they are filled in by means of lace, closely gathered silk, or chiffon. Care should be taken that the " filling" is warmly lined. The few new materials which have made their appear- ance are all spotted. Not dark on light, as W3B the case when last spots were "in," but light on dark, such as dark grey spotted with white, or black with mauve. Plain black silk in the softer makes, such as bengaline, gros-grain. Irish peplin,and faille, promise to be much worn, to the exclusion of glace, of which we are some- what weary —while clips-dr-chine seems likely to be used by the furlong for afternoon and evening gowns. Though the tiny Empire fan is particularly mcdish just now, the one built of long ostrich feathers, with its sticks as rich or as plain as may be desired, is by no means in the background. Her Majesty Queen Alexandra distinctly approves of the latter variety,than which none are more stately. The Princess of Wales, the Duchess of Argyll, and the Duchees of Portland are all ardent tan admirers, and possess among their collec- tions many rare specimens. No praise can be too high for the pictures of Irish life elven us in Miss Katherine Tynan's " A Union of Hearts." This distinguished authoress has caught exactly the spirit of the Irish, their humour and tender- t:ogs, their ready revolt against a suspicion of tyranny, and their fierce love of their country. All the characters are cleverly drawn, and the story so well told that one finds it difficult indeed to lay aside the fascieating book until the last page is reached. -

JACK KISSED HER.

Jack—don't you envy him ?—kissed her While taking a walk in the square— So sweet she, he couldn't resist her. And ought she, you fancy, to care? And should sbe be mad as a hatter? Or should she have given it back? Or should she pass over the matter. And say: "Oh, 'twas Jack; only Jack ! " Is the hue of her cheeks indication Of furious auger, or rot ? Or due to some slight irritation O'er choosing so public a spot? LWere she kissed in the hall, would she mildly Pret,st : "Please be good—there's papa !" And is she now speeding on, wildly, To seek her protecting mamma ? Is Jack to be blamed for his action ? Are you holding hina chep in disgrace? Supposing he saw a di.traction Of " Try, if you like," on her face ! I'll add, as the close of my ditty, Ere spinsters regard her as lost, And grasips deplore: "What a pity !" That Jack—clever Jack—was Jack Frost ! EDWIN L. SABIN. IN FEBRUARY SMART SET.

During the play of the Christian's Cross,in the Queen's Hall, Castleford, last night, Mrs Thema' Smith, of the Engineers Inn, Carlton-street, Castleford, was seized with a fit, and died almost immediately. A ,ctoolboy named M'Ginley (13), residing at Blair- gowrie, is in the hands of the police. It is alleged he found a ewe entangled in a bramble bush. Taking a hatchet he knocked out its brains, and then unobserved carried it to his mother's house, where be cut it up with a table knife, and was attempting to ekin it when his mother returned.

FATAL FALLS AT WARRINGTON.

'Strange Treatment of an Injured

Woman.

Mr S Brighouse conducted inquests a; the Warring- ton Police Station on Tuesday afternoon relative to two deaths resulting from falls. The first case inquired into was that concerning the death of Edward Norman, deputy lodging-house keeper, 34, Rose and Crown-st. Ralph Norman, deceased's son, stated that his father was sixty-seven years of age. On Wednesday,the Mud inst,deceased complained ot having a bad arm, telling witness that be had fallen initheileitchen and cut it. On the following day he was removed to the Workhouse hospital and died on Friday. Previous to the Wednes- day deceased had never mentioned anything about hav- ing received an injury. Margaret Kermode, wife of Henry Kermode,proprietor of the lodging house at 34, Rose and Crown-street, sad deceased had acted as deputy for them for the past three years. During the past three months be had been very much andicted to drink. On Monday, January 20th, he complained of being ill and remained in bed all that day and also on Tuesday and Wednesday. He be- came worse on Wednesday at d told witness that his arm was bad,and that be fell down in the kitchen acme time , ago and cut it, Nurse Marsland said' deceased was admitted to the Workhouse Hospital on the previous Thursday and died on the following night. The Coroner read a letter from Dr Braide, the Union Medical Officer, stating that the cause of death was selulitis and blood poisoning, following on the neglect of an injury to the arm. A verdict of " Accidental death " was returned. The jury then inquired into the death of Sarah Cotton, aged sixty-one, who lodged at 26, Watkin-street. Evidence was given by Elizabeth Bentley, with whom the deceased lodged, to the effect that on Saturday, January 186, deceased left the kitchen to retire to bed, and when she was half way upstairs she fell backwards to the bottom. She was picked up and placed on a sofa. She did not speak afterwards and lay on the sofa without medical attention until Monday morning, when witness sent for deceased's sister. Margaret Jane Hynes, deceased's sister, said that When she went to the house of Mrs Bentley on Monday she found deceased lying on the sofa unconscious. She sent for Dr Nuttall. who ordered deceased's removal Is the Infirmary where she died. The jury returned a verdict of Accidental death." At their request the Coroner censured Mrs Bentley for her conduct in allowing deceased to lie on the sofa until Monday without attention. Mr Brighouse, in doing so, said the only thing he could say was that he did not think there was a pew n in existence with so little brains as to act as Mrs Bentley had done. If people were poor they must have medical attention, just as it they were in better circumstances.

ThE LATE MR. W. CHANTRY.

At St Paul's Church, on Sunday morning, the organ- ist (Mr E Wilkes, A.L.C.M) played the Dead March in " Saul" as a mark of respect to the late Mr Chautry. In the evening the Vice, spoke in kindly terms of the deceased's work in connection with the church and parish. He was, Mr Tarbuck observed, his first warden and when asked to continuo his duties be consented to serve for another year as Vicar's warden although he bad been in office a long time. Be was wonderfully enthusiastic as to the growth of the work in the parish and his text was " Patience and perseverance will accomplish as much as you desire." He (the vicar), should never forget the great debt of gratitude he owed to Mr Chantey, and he was sure the congregation would join with him in expressing sympathy with the bereaved widow and family. The funeral took place in St. Paul's cherchyard on Tuesday afternoon, and a large gathering of relatives and friends afforded striking evidence of the respect entertained for the deceased. HALLIWELL SUTCLIFFE " The Zieventh Comtnandmegt." fel.)

PART IL

The Major, when he and Oswald met the ',inext morning, was a little ashamed of his overnightcon- fession ; be was therefore a hundredfold more resolved to make no overtures of peace to the little old lady who inhabited Linden Cotta ,ire. So Wynne departed to his painting in much tribulation of heart: the missing factor In this country paradise pressed upon him more and more heavily as time went on; he was one quarter in love with the Unknown already by reason of her prettiness, and quite half in love with her because she seemed so unattainable: the odd quarter could easily be made up by aid of a little propinquity. " It is too ridiculous," he muttered, sale worked in it fresh bit of background to his picture ; "she at one end of the village, I at the other: the only bar to cur knowing each otheris a flimsy quarrel bet- ween two peopie old enough to know better. If I were in a book, now, I should perform some feat of valour—bull, cow, running water, any country phe- nomenon would 'serve —but that always was the weak pal tof life, to my thinking; we are not in books." Ile sought a neighbouring farmhouse about noon; a mighty hunger and a mightier thirst were upon him, and he decided to have lunch then and there if he could obtain it. From the farm-wife he gleaned a scrap of ioeal history which seemed to him unim- portant etionkh at the time. Three years before, it seemed, the oniet neighbourhood of Traviaton had been visited by a curious atmospheric disturbance; ;t abont thirty seconds by the clock, but its life was as merry as it was short : without the least warainir, a kina of 4,inglicised tornado sprang out of the }mart of a sunny, cloudless sky, and carried everything before it. The roof of the barn attached to this very farm had been lifted clear away to a distanee of two field-lengths haystacks were scattered abroad ; trees came crashing earthwards on every hand.

" That it 4 the sort of festivity I am yearning for," thought Oswald Wynne, as he munched his cheese and bread.

A tv-14t, it seems. may be father to a tornado, as the artist was destined to learn.

Three days later, when the Major's will WAS as stern as ever in the matter of peace overtures, and after the nephew had watched the play of the sun- light on the Unknown's yellow hair throughout a long country service, it occurred to Oswald Wynne that ha would no for an afternoon's ride ; hope de- ferred had mad; him almost feverish, and 'rigorous exercise was needed to blow away the cobwebs. "I may not be back to dinner, uncle; you won't mind once in_ a_ way ?" _ _ _

" Oh, no, I shan't mind there are few things I enjoy better than a solitary dinner," retorted the uncle, " 141mre are you ofi to, boy? Up to some mischief or other, I'll wager."

" I want some fresh' air in niv lungs, that is all. Perhap.3, I shall get as far as Lewisford ; if rdo, mean to put up at that jolly old inn there and have some dinner. I'll be back in good time. Good- bye." He turned ou reaching the gate. " be began. "Well, sir, well? Conscience pricking you because you era leaving the old man alone ?" " No—it isn't quite that—are you sure that nothing can be done—Miss Penderby—" "Confound Miss Penderby ! Be off for your ride," snapped the Major. as he went indoors and banged the door behind him. "Confound Major Pordham, say I," muttered the nephew ruefully. After an early dinner at the Lewisford inn, Oswald Wynne rode homewards at an easy trot. He told himself, with the certainty of a well-lined inner man, that the pretty stranger was of the slightest consequence, and that he intended to enjoy the late- May evening as a free man should. He reached the high wall which skirted the road on this side Traviston ; the mare had 'dropped to a walk- ing pace, and the rider's thoughts were busy with his uncompleted picture. Both mare and rider were roused from dreamland by a curious, rushing sound, by the nose of splintering branches, by the shell) note of a girl's scream. _ _ . . .

" P 4. Jove, I believe it the tornado of three years ago, come to revive old memories," muttered Wynne.

The mare plunged a little, but the high wall on their right had screened them from any direct attack on the part of the wind, and Wynne speedily succeeded in paeffying her. On turning a corner of the road, Oiwald. Wynne became conscious of a bewildering mixture of white dress, yellow hair and green haw- thorn twigs: he jumped out of the saddle, hitched the bridle to an adjacent gate-post and diffidently approached the girl. "I—l'm afraid you are in difficulties," he observed. "How singularly clever of you to jump to such a conclusion," retorted the Unknown, brusquely. She was con.cious that the spectaole must be ludicrous in the extreme for an onlooker

" What has happened exactly: was it the wind?' " I suppose it 'WAS. I was walking quietly on the road—there was not enough wind to raise the dust— a whirlwind suddenly came down and—and the next thing I remember is being scratched by a lot of sharp thorns, and feeling myself triable to move, and find- ing I was quite in the middle of the hedge." "Perhaps you are more interested in how you are to get out again than how you got in." "Of course I ; I wondered if that point of view would strike you presently. These thorns are hurt- ing me abominably." It was not an easy task that Oswald Wynne had set himself, but he went to work manfully ; in a few minutes the girl was standing safe, though scarcely sound, on the white high-road. " My dress is in tatters," she remarked, dismally. " So are your hands, which are more important I can't tell you how sorry I am—" " Please don't trouble. Dare I go through the village like this !" A happy thought struck Oswald Wynne. " would suggest—" he began. " What would you suggest ?" "—That you stop at my uncle's on the way— Major Fordham, you know—his house is not a hun- dred yards of." " How absurd you are. I have not the privilege of Major Fordham's acquaintance—and, if I had, I should scareely present myself in this guise, should I ?"

"Why not? It will be dusk in an hour or so, and —on can (Teen home usQbse:.•ra.3-" " IT°, Tinfl gti straight to LooUen Cottage, ana the village can think what it likes." Oswald Wynne regarded her with a look of the utrcost gravity. " Aro you fond of curry ?" asked.

"Am I—fond—of—curry ?" repeated the girl in surprise. Then she laughed. " I had forgotten for the moment. So you know the story ?" "Yes—not the same story, probably, but the other side of it. Doesn't it strike you as absurd that peo- ple should quarrel for life on such slender provoca. tion ?"

"Ridiculous ; but the Major does seem to have been very rude to Miss Penderby." "And Miss Penderby does seem to have tried the Major's constitution severely. They might cry quits."

" But they never will. Miss Penderby—" "Exactly; and the Xajor.—But the younger gen- eration has more sense ; that was really why I sug- gested your dropping in on the Major; between us we could overcome him in a very short time." " Oh, but I couldn't drop in 'as you call it No, I must make the best of my way home." " May I escort you so far? The least I can do is to see you safely through the mishap." "You are very kind; of course, under the cir- cumstances, I can offer no objections. Perhaps you will come in for a moment, that Miss Penderby may thank you for your help." But they did not get beyond the boundary-hedge of the Major's garden, after all. The sound of voices came to them from the other aide of the hedge, and both stopped instinctively. " The Major has one guest already," said Wynne.'• "Miss Penderby is the guest. That was unmis- takeably her voice," said the girl. They looked at each other in amazement. "How on earth—has she so far relented as to call—"

" Impossible, unless the Major has previously been to Linden Cottage to apologise. Nothing less would satisfy the dear old lady." " And nothing was further from my uncle's thoughts, I know. It is very odd." Oswald Wynne, overcome by the strangeness of the situation, behaved as a village urchin might be expected to do. He made a little hole in the hedge with the butt of his riding whip, put his eye thereto, and eagerly scanned the lawn. "Impossible or not," he said at last, abandoning his post, " they are talking as fast as they can go, and Miss Penderby is seated in a garden-chair as ocanfortably as if she had been born there."

A cigar was between his lips, and a sporting novel lay on his knees. Ho was not reading, however ; his eyes rested meditatively on a bush of guelder roses growing in the middle of the hedge that separated the garden from the road ; he was talking to him- self, after the manner of gentlemen of uncertain

"Now, about that girl of Oswald's. The young cub doesn't say much, but I fancy he's pretty hard hit. All restlessness ; now at this now at that ; off for a ride and a dinner the Lord knows where, when he can find better food at home—yes and better soc- iety, too, confound him ! He read a page of his novel, muttered a little, then recommenced his musings, his eyes again fixed on the guelder-roses.

At the moment when the Major strolled out of doors for a smoke on the lawn, a little old lady had strolled out of her door and into her well-stocked garden. She was clad in a parlous gown of pea- cock-blue; a weather-streaked sun-bonnet, trimmed with purple ribbons, did its best to hide her pretty white hair. Directly she emerged into the fading light, she elevated a white umbrella and interposed it between the dyin,a, sun and her quaint little face; for Letitia Penderby was justly proud of her com- plexion, and she knew not what ravages even an in- nocent sunset might work upon its creamy delicacy. The sun-umbrella, in fact, offered a sure index to Miss Penderby's two most pronounced characteris- tics : the upper part bore witness to her femininity, the handle testified to a certain vigour which was the growth of later years. It was thick and tough, this handle ; it had a vindictive knob at the top ; Its purpose, Miss Letitia would have told you, was to annihilate any rude mate who ventured to as- sault her during the long country rambles which were her delight. True, the rude male had failed to putin an appearance hitherto, but one could never be sure, and that little old lady had a grim deter- mination, should oecasion serve, to ply a lusty arm. She little guessed that this strength of handle and framework was destined to make itself the instru- ment of peace. Miss Letitia dawdled among her flowers to- night, chattering vaguely to herself as ladies of un- certain years will chatter. . . . _

" I an sure the girls of to-day are different from what we used to be. Fanny is a dear girl, but she seems singularly wanting in—what shall I call it? —in reserve. Fifty years ago a young girl would not have dreamed of being interested in any young man until they had become acquainted with each other. Dear me, and an artist too ! There is some- thing dreadfully indelicate about the idea of an artist, though 1 cannot say why ; whether it is that the models—. But how naughty of dear Fanny to induce me to tell her so much about the Major, and then to suggest that /—I, Letitia Penderby—should take the first stop towerds a reconciliation. Not that it was right of her, or maidenly, or in any way decorous, when she had seen the artist coming out of Major Fordham's gate—oh, yes, she confessed as much it was not her place to endeavour to make the Major's acquaintance. Indeed, I should be sorry to introduce any ady to the Major ; he is not to be trusted ; who knows but what, after that dreadful evening when he forgot himself so—f3o miserably, he may break out again at any time? But I fear the Major has schooled himself far too long to believe himself in the right ever to—" Miss Letitia Penderby, instead of finishing her sen- tence, opened the green and white gate, and went out into the street. It may seem curious that her way led past the Major's garden, but this was mere- ly a coincidence. Fanny had spoken of returning this way after her walk, and it was a little late for the dear girl to be out of doors, unprotected by the sun-umbrella

And this brings us back to the Major, seated in a deck chair with his eyes fixed absently on his gar- den hedge. The old gentleman was roused from his musings by a succession of events, too numerous to tabulate and too rapid to admit of their being dis- Antangle.d one from the other. The gadder-rose tree suddenly began bowing to him in most boisterous fashion ; the chimney-pots from a neighbour's house across the way came to replace those which had already vanished from the Major's cottage, and, failing to drop properly in position, smashed themselves to bits on the roof ; the sky seemed to be shouting. But those things were trifling ; a vaster, a more awe-inspiring phenomenon than them all was riveting the Major's attention. Above the boundary hedge rose a vision of white and peacock-blue ; it poised itself in mid-air, straight above the place where the Major was staring up with eyes too wonder-stricken to look afraid ; it came to earth again, and stood not three feet from the Major's chair, and dropped incontinently into a neighbour- ing seat. A deep silence succeeded to the storm, deeper by reason of the many things which two people were leaving all unsaid. Miss Letitia Penderby, always attentive to pro- priety before permitting herself to feel alarm, smoothed out her skirts and drew two diminutive feet well under shelter. She trusted the Major had not _noticed that brief half-second of an ankle.

Major Fordham coughed perplexedly. He had once—the Indian climate is so trying—he had once before seen visions that had nothing to do with the outside world; could it be possible that—? " I—l beg your pardon,', he observed at length. Miss Letitia felt that she stood in need of all her dignity. "I did not venture a remark," she re- sponded, drawing herself up severely. "r—certainly not. Er—very warm for the tune of the year, is it not?" "Very warm," in icy tones. A long pause. "There seems to have been a little breeze just now. An accident over which I had little control—"

" My dear Madam, I am charmed that good for- tune has compelled you to seek my hospitality." "That is like the old Arthur who used to—but I must not think of that," murmured Letitia Pen- derby. She was astounded at the sense of home with which the garden affected her; it was growing diffi- cult to maintain her frigid air. " Bless my soul," murmured the Major, • " but I never fully realised what it means to be without Letitia. If she would but abandon that chilly atti- tude of hers, who knows—" "You will doubtless pardon the length of ray stay," said Miss Penderby at last, rising from her chair. " I found myself a little—a little short of breath: but lam quite recovered now. Allow me to thank you, and to wish you good-night." She half held out one cotton-gloved hand. The Major grew desperate and threw prudence to the winds. He caught the hand, and pulled off the glove, and pressed his lips to the tapering fingers. Miss Letitia trembed and grew faint, then recovered herself.

The Major paused and wiped his brow. It was very hard to give up the cherished conviction of twelve months. But he stole a glance at Miss Letitia, and it was all over with him. But then— but then—you were always impulsive, Arthur, and then perhaps—I—was a little at fault, too, about that curry." For at this mo- ment a girl with glorious yellow hair and a tattered white dress, and a young man, whose gravity was considerably overdone, had turned the corner of the lilac-path and were bearing down upon the garden chairs.

He had a task before him, and it was useless to postpone it until another day. " Oswald," he said, with dignity, "come and be presented to Miss Letitia Penderby, my—my pro. raised wife. "Well, upon my soul—here, I say, confound it—the cool impertinence of the boy," sputtered the Major under his breath.

A FRUIT DEALER'S FAITH.

Rewarded at Blackburn.

Faith is one of our cardinal virtues, and when rightly ,pplied is productive of much good and happiness. An nteresting story of faith rewarded was related the other day by Mr J B Kenyon, 22, Malvern-avenue, Blfckbure. This gentleman, who was formerly a book-keeper, is now a fish and fruit dealer, mad the Etry of his faith was related to a "Blackburn Standard and Express" reporter.

1`1.,a,t Angart holidays," he said in answer to a query, " I was affi,ctrd with an abscess which caused me a great deal of trout le, but I must candidly confess that the doctor, who attended me for it effectually cured me. The matter, however, did net end there, tor I was lett in a very weak condition." " And did ym continue to take medioal advice ?" asked the reporter. "Yen, but I did not improve, and my weakness con- tinued just the same. I felt weak and languid, and the slightest exertion greatly tired me. I felt unfit for any kind of work, and felt greatly distressed that 'ime did not seem to give me back coy strength. Then I remembered that two or three years ago I had tried Dr Williams' pink pills for pale peeple and they then did me good. I determined to give them another trial, so thought a box from Mr Allsupp, chemist, ollln Bank." "What was the ri suit ?"

"After the first week I was another man altogether, my streneth and energy returning daily, until I was soon all right. I took about three boxes of the pills in ,11."

" What is your state Of health now 1" am as strong as usual and abh to carry On my business without feeling done up or exhausted." " Do you attribute your cure to Dr pink Pi's P" " Yes," earnestly replied Mr Kenyon, and his answer should be carefully noted. "I find they build me up better than any thing else. They are a good tonic, in my opinion, and I don't mind what I tell you being pub- I have recommended the pills to several people." Yee," interjected Moil Kenyon," I k iow two young ladies, one of whom was under medical advice for many years without any signs of improvement,but they after- wards took Dr Williams' pink pills and both derived a great deal of rood from them. Speaking for myself, I can tell you I have taken over • sovereign's worth and my perseverance has been amply rewarded in the good effect the pills have had on ray health." " Some time ago," addeu Mr Kenyon. " we both had influenza and Dr Williams' pink pills brmght us round quickly. I believe that if anyone with a cold will try toe pills they will be cured. I have grest faith in the curative properties of the pills, which only rEquire to be personally known to be appreciated."

Mrs Kenyon, who was present during the interview corroborated her husband's story, and was just as en- thusiastic a believer in the virtues of the pilis as her husband.

THE CORONATION

OYAL PROCESSIOX

(THE ROUTE IS INDICATED BY A DOTTED LINE.)

Now that the first anniversary of his Majesty's accession has come and gone, and the Coronation session of Parliament is well under way, the arrangements for the great event itself are begin. uing to get into shape, and people are more able to make their plans for seeing the sights of the historic third week in June. Coronation Day proper—Le. the day of the service in Westminster Abbey, the taking of the oaths by his Majesty, and the blessing and crowning by the Archbishops—will be June 26th, and con- trary to expectation, the historic procession round the streets of London will take place the day after—the 27th—instead of the day before—the 25th—but, upon consideration, it is clear to all that it is more fitting that a crowned King should show himself to his people than an uncrowned one. Another surprise to the public, and not such a pleasant one, is the selected route, which is, after all, to be identical with that of the Diamond Jubilee procession in 1897. This decision came as a distinctly unexpected piece of news, as all had thought that the route would either be that used by the Prince and Princess of Wales in their journey to the City in the first week of December, or practically the same, Regent Street and dxford Street being substituted for Shaftesbury Avenue. Great chagrin is felt by the shopkeepers and -window owners on these highways of traffic, who had counted on big "lets," but this is in a way compensated for by the delight of those who have similar advantages on the selected route. However, though following precedent, the choice of the Strand approach to the City is certainly not so r with the public in general, as the Regent Street way is not only the more imposing, but would afford More room for the man in the street to watch the procession from. The rejected route, which was looked upon as settled, subject to his Majesty's approval, had actually been mapped out and tentatively traversed by the police and House- hold officials, who made copious notes and great plans, thinking that it was the way which would finally have to be dealt with. Fuller details are also to hand of the order of the procession itself, and here, too, precedent will be adhered to, but space precludes any detailed mention of the positions, of the various celebrities. Most of the offices have by now been allotted, and shortly the Court of Claims and the House of Lords will decide the more important ot the doubtful cases, which require more investigation than the others. EVery- thing tends to show what a great affair the' Coronation will be, and between now and then it is is sure to be the main topic of discussion.

The capture of General Ben Viljoen and Commandant Hans Botha, especially the former, should have, as Lord Kitchener hopes, a con- siderable effect on the enemy still in the field to the north of the Eastern Railway, and we are also justified in believing that it will have some effect upon the whole of Botha's forces. General Viljoen was one of the most iui- portant and use- ful leaders left to the Boers, and his loss at this stage of the War Will be almost if not quite irre- par abl e. Al- though he has not ranked with De Wet, Delarey, and one or two others he has nevertheless been exceedingly ac- tive ever since • the outbreak of the War, and he has proved a most trouble- some individual to the British. In

October 1899, he fought at Elands Laagte and was reported killed, but the report of his death was contradicted a few days later. His capture it noteworthy in a political as well as in a fighting sense. Before the War he was the fire-eater of the Volksraad, and the most notorious anti-British member of that assembly. He has an unenviable reputation for braggadocio, and is a man of coarse manners. In Johannesburg, before; the War, he showed his hatred of the English in every way he could think of, and made himself particularly offensive. Since he became Generill Louis Botha't Lieutenant, after the flight of Mr. Kruger and the adoption of guerilla methods of warfare, his favourite fighting ground has been the Middleburg district, but he has also been heard of in othei quarters Two of his most distinguished achieve. ments were the capture of the British post at Helvetia, and the attack on the Australians at Wilmansrust, when •he secured two hundred prisoners and two pom-poms. Like De Wet and athers he has proved himself a master in the art of eluding our columns, who have beer searching for and pursuing him for months and it was was only by accident that some of our intelligence officers finally lit upon him while looking for another troublesome Boei eommander.

IHE EXAMINEE. FRID le, JANUARY 31 1902. THE FREE CHURCHES. I WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Appointment of --Workhouse Master and Matron.

REV. Z. G. GREENHOIIGII, ILA.

This popular Baptist minister is due to arrive in England from his extended tour in Australia at the end of this month. Mr. Greenhough visited the Antipodes in a dual capacity —as the representative of the Baptist Union at the jubilee celebrations of the denomination in Australia, and as the representative of the Free Church Federation. His return has been delayed on account of his having to make a second visit to Ceylon. Mr. Greenhough will take a prominent part at the coming meetings of the National Free Church Council at Bradford. As the retiring Presi- dent, he will preach the opening sermon, and he will also give an aacount, at a special session, of his travel experiences.

A meeting of this Board was held in the Board Room at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Mr A J Jolley presided, and there were also present :—Mrs Clay. Rev Father Wilson, Rev J H Riodette, Dr Patterson. Messrs William Hickmanf T H Ashcroft, J Billings, 8 Davies, J Clarke, I Payton, J Robinson, J Backhouse T Ritchie, William Houghton, J Pilling, R Fershaw, Crocks, CI Mather, A Jolley, J Ball, J W Knowleo, Halton, with J C Sutton (clerk), C H Ward (master), A Wright (superintendent Padgata Schools). RESIGNATION OF A NURSE. At a meeting of the HOLM Committee,the resignation of nurse McCormick was accepted and Miss E Colts was appointed at a salary of £25 per annum with rations uniform and the usual allowanc's.—lt was recom- mended that the salary of nurse Watson be increased I rom £3O to £35 per annum,and that the salary of Miss Kelly, the cook, be increased, from £2O to £22 10s per annum in addition to superannuation allowance. Mr BROOKS moved,and Mrs CLAY seconded the con- firmation of the minutes and they were agreed to. THE:TREATMENT OF SMALLPDX IN WORKHOUSES. A communication from the Statistical Department of the Local Government Board with reference to relief in kind during the half-year ending Mashaelmas, 1901,was read, from which it appeared that the cost was returned as follows :—Mr Garnett's districts, £BO 10a lid ' • Mr Aukland's, £9 5s 5i ; Mr Bowes's £5B 183 441.—A letter was read from the Local Government Board approfing of the appointment of Miss FL Wagstaffe as probationer nurse. Circular letters had also beau re ceived from the Local Government Boar- w ith reference to the supply of lymph t public vaecinators, and drawing attention to the public notices to be given of the changes made in the arrange- ments for vaccioation.—The Clerk statal that there was a great demand for lymph in consequence of the out break of smallpox but be did not anticipate any silort- age in the supply. It was asked that great care should be exercised in the case of casual paupers to prevent the introduction of smallpox. Only circumstances of grave urgency justified the admission or retention of a smallpox care in a workhouse,and measures in such a case should be taken to have all the inmates and per- sons employed to attend on the patient vaccinated. He (Mr Sutton) bad written to Mrs Ward, the inatron,and the Medioal Officer calling their attention to the matter. CHRISTMAS BEER TO INMATES. The 7CLERK said he had received a letter from the Gene al Secretary of the United Temperance Council, asking if the Board allowed alcoholic drinks to be con- sumed by the inmates of the Workhouse at Christman. Mr Knowoes : To be taken by the members ? (Laughter.) Mr ALFRED JOLLEY Which of them? (Renewed laughter.) The CLERK said he had replied to the letter stating that such of the male inmates as chose to take it were allowed one pint each and the females half a pint each. WARNING TO OVERSEERS. The CLERK stated that, as usual, the overseers of Rixton had not paid their calls. (Laughter.) It was no laughing matter, and he asked permission to take summonses out against them. The CHAIRMAN moved that, in case of any defaulting parish in the union, tho Clerk be empowered to take immediate action. (Hear, hear.) Mr A Joheny seconded, and the motion was unani- mously carried. LUNACY IN THE WARRINGTON UNION. The Cortex presented a return ef pauper lunatics chargeable on Ist January,l9o2, distinguishing between patients settled in the Administrative County of Lan- caster and in the County Borough of Warriogton, Mr Sutton added : " It is satisfactory to observe, having, regard to the great increase in the number of lunatics in the several asylums in the county, that while the re- turn shows as compared frith the list for Ist January, 1901, an increase of one at Prestwich, two at Haydook Lodge, and one at Burntwood, there was a decrease on January let at Rainhill of ten, Whittinghana four, and Lancaster one, showing a net decrease of eleven. Since, January lit however by the opening of Winwick Asy- lum, six lanaties have been removed from the Work- la -use to the Rainhill Asylum. It may be fairly claimed that, as in previous years,the number chargeable to this union has been kept down by careful attention given to the transfer of chst,geability of non-settled lunatics." THE FURNISIiIING OF THE NEWTON RELIEF OFFICES. Mr BlLLillon said the Newtm Re;ief Committee, with its usual courtesy, desired to receive from the Board authority to ask for estimates and make contracts for the furnishing of the new relief offices at Earlestown and also for the supply and fitting up of blinds in the house part as well as in the Board room. He moved that the Newton Relief Committee be empowered to determine contracts for the work, Dr PATTERSON seconded the proposition. Mr KNOWLES said the Beard once discovered that the Newton Relief Committee was fond of spending a lot of money. (Laughter.l He thought It would be well to put a practical man on the Newton Committee to assist them in the furnishing business. He would suggest that Mr Hiokman's name be added to the committee for the work mentioned. Mr J Beer. seconded. Mr HICKMAN said he was afraid he would require someone else to accompany him. He would be very much alone on the Newton Committee and would require someone to back him up. The CHAIRMAN said he was quite sure that whatever bull:has they had to transact would net be of a bellicose but of a decisive character, and would be conducted with consummate wisdom. (Hear, hear.) The proposition was then agreed to as was also the suggestion that Mr Hickman's name be added to the Newton Csmmittee. APPOINTMENT OF MASTER AND MATRON. At this stage of the proceedings, the reporters were asked to retire whilst the Board proceeded to consider the appointment of master and matron. The selected candidates for the position were: Mr and Mrs H D Garnham, master and matron rof the joint workhouse, West Derby Union; Mr and Mrs Fred Gate, master and matron of Dawsbury Workhouse; Mr and Mrs G W Lloyd, assistant master and matron of Hull Workhouse; Mrs Mary Ward, the present matron of the Warrington Workhouse and Mr John Barron (22)unmarried,mester's senior clerk, Prescot Union.—All the candidates sp-eared before the Board with tho exception of Mr Mr and Mrs Lloyd, of Hull, who withdrew, having in the meantime acc.pted another appointment. After carefully considering the applications, the Board decided to appoint Mr and Mrs Gate, of Dewsbury to the position. The joint salary is £l7hi per annum, witn emoluments amounting to a further sum of£l2o,making £295 altogether. Mr Gate is 34 years of age and has been four years assistant master and genen years master at Dewsbury Workhouse. Prior to her marriage, Mrs Gate was children's attendant at Sheffield for six years, matron at Hastings Union for :one year and matron St Pancras Union, one andlhalf a years. This was all the business.

DR. TOWNSEND,

who is to succeed Mr. Greenhough in the Presi- dential chair, is not so well known outside his own denomination as some other Presidents have been. He is a distinguished and honoured minister of the Methodist New Connexion, which, despite its name, is the oldest of the secessions from Wesleyan Methodism. Dr. Townsend, who has private means, is this year, at his own request, without a charge, and lives in a pretty house amid charming scepery at Prestatyn, North Wales. He edits the Metho- dist New Connexion .Magazine, and among hit' monthly contributions are some excellent brief reviews of new books. Dr. Townsend is a natural orator, and great things are expected of him at Bradford. There is no hint at present as to who is likely to be nominated as his sue- ces4or.

===== 0002601_19020131_art0039.txt | included=true | included =====
'MR. PRICE HUGHES.

The Rev. Hugh Price Hughes, M.A., is still at Bournemouth, making good progress towards recovery. Although refraining from public engagements, Mr. Hughes is very busy, and does a great deal of work for his paper, the Methodist Times. His leading articles and notes on current events have never been more vigorous. The February number of the British Monthly publishes a striking picture of Mr. Hughes in Court dress, from a private photograph, taken at the time when Mr. Hughes, President of the Wesleyan Conference, was presented to her late Majesty. Mr. Hughes expects to attend the annual meeting of the National Free Church Council at Bradford in March.

A POPULAR ROOK. The "autobiography of Gipsy Smith is still "booming." It is now in its third edition (seventh thousand) and continues to sell rapidly. No one is more surprised at the success of the volume than Mr. Smith himself. When the writing of the volume was first suggested to him, he asked: "And do you really think that there has been enough of interest in my life to make a book P" His doubts and fears have been proved to be ground- less. Mr. Smith's latest venture, his "Inquiry Department" in the Free Church Chronicle, is quickly becoming very popular.

"HARRY LINDSAY "

Mr. H. Lindsay Hudson ("Harry Lindsay ") seems to be the most industrious of the Metho- dist story writers at present. He has just published, through Messrs. Chatto and Windus, & historical story, "Judah Pyecroft, Puritan,* and is busy on a long story dealing with reli- gion and commercial life. Mr. Lindsay's sue- cessor as editor of the Methodist Weekly is Mr. J. Smart. Readers of the Methodist papers eagerly welcome the return of "Historicus (Mr. W. M. Crook) to the columns of the Metho- dist Times. Mr. Cook differed toto cedo from Mr. Price Hughes on the question of the war, and • his contributions were suspended for a considerable period. The subject of the war is still for him taboo.

===== 0002601_19020131_art0040.txt | included=true | included =====
RUDIOURS.

The Rev. R. J. Campbell, 8.A., is mentioned as a likely successor to the Rev. F. B. Meyer, 13. A., at Christ Church, Westminster-bridge- road, S.E. No definite steps have yet been taken. Mr. Meyer's resignation does not take effect until September. Mr. Campbell is also spoken of in connection with the vacancy at Trinity Congregational Church, 'Glasgow, caused by the transference of Dr. John Hunter to London. There is no whisper at present of a successor to the Rev. G. Clampbell Morgan at New Court Chapel, Tollington-park.

A GREAT PREACHER'S NERVOUSNESS.

For the first time during a period of between fifty and sixty yoars Dr. Alexander McLaren, of Manchester, has been unable to preach his annual evening sermon to young pe.ople. This famous preacher, despite his long experience and his assured position, still suffers acutely at times from nervousness, becomes, indeed, as the phrase is, a bundle of nerves. It was on this ground that he intimated his inability to preach the customary Berman. He told his morning congregation that strain and excitement pre- vented him from delivering a discourse in the evening.

===== 0002601_19020131_art0041.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE DR. KILLEN.

Dr. Killen, the eminent Irish Presbyterian, had all but reached his hundredth year. A very good story used to be told of it. His brother James, who was a pugnacious and die- imitations man, published a book entitled "Our Friends in Heaven." "I am so glad to know," said pr. Killen, "that our James has friends in that quarter. He has certainly very few down hero below."

"P. W. W."

"P'. W. W.," whose excellent notes on the Churches in the Daily News have attracted con- siderable attention, is Mr. P. Whitwell Wilson. He is, we believe, a graduate of Cambridge, and lives at Twickenham. Mr. Wilson is a Primitive Methodist.

===== 0002601_19020131_art0042.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHURCH PAPERS AND NONCONFORMITY.

The Chtrch papers have of late been devot- ing considerable attention to Nonconformist matters. The Guardian of January 15 contained an interesting article on "The National Council of Evangelical Free Churches," written by a Nonconformist correspondent. Some people shrewdly suspect that this Nonconformist is Mr. Howard Kennedy, of the Times, a son of the late Reverend Dr. Kennedy, Congregational minister at Stepney. The tone of the article is friendly. But what does the writer mean by describing Mr. R. W. Perks, M.P., as an ally of the Church of England? Does he mean to suggest that the loyalty to Nonconformity of the originator of the Million Guineas Fund has declined? Nothing could be more prepos- terous. And it is quite the reverse Of truth to describe the National Council's opposition to the Government's education policy as academic.

,Warrington. , The New Coroner's Court.

The Coroner's Court at t-h-e-;1;w-Voiice Buildings has now been completed and is undoubtedly admir- ably adapted for the purposes for which it is intend ed. The Police Court is now undergoing several alterations and the magistrates have sat in the Coroner's Court daily since Tuesday. The room is splendidly furnished and the acoustic properties are all that cam be desired.

Ragged School and Mission.

The schora—rs of the above school were enter- tained to tea on Saturday,by Mr Roger C Parr, about 350 being present. After tea Mr Parr interested the children with his popular lecture entitled " My travels through Japan," illustrated with 120 lantern slides, being photographs taken by Mr Parr while on tour. Caere were also present the Mayoress and Miss Parr and Mrs and Miss Willis. A hearty vote of thanks was passed to Mr Parr for his kind- ness in giving the tea and for his lecture. On leaving the room each person received an orange. On Monday evening the third Band of Hope enter- tainment was held in the school. Mrs H Roberts presided and spoke a few encouraging words to the children. A capital programme was gone through by the membercconsisting of songs, solos, and reci- tations, and it concluded with a dialogue entitled "Miss Shaw's Band of Hope Class."

the morning all the year round and perambuleting the streets until late at night, endeavouring to sell their wares. His lordship felt it was a sad pity that so much young life should be allowed to run to waste, many of the girls yielding to temptation and, alas, entering the ranks of the criminal. His lordship felt that if this life could be directed into a right channel it might become beneficial to the community at large as well as to the girl' and women themselves, so in 1866 this institution was established and centres were formed in various parts of London for the gathering in of -these girls and women. After thirty-six years of labour, the work which was begun in a very small and humble way, had by the goodness of God extended so that it had become one of the largest charities of London. They had their own orphanage which accommodated two hundred and fifty fatherless and motherless little girls, whom they received without any question of age, creed or physical infirmity. The youngest children they received were two twin sisters who were only ten days old when their mother died, leaving nine chil- dren unprovided for. Children were taken to this orphanage until they were sixteen years of age and then they were placed out in domestic service, the linstitution continuing to exercise a parental over- sight over them. There was also a cottage hospitel and a home for blind and crippled children. The society had also established a breakfast room where the women and girls who frequented the markets from the early morning were supplied with a pint of cocoa and two slices of bread and butter. Tneir cases were also inquired into and help was given as fer as possible. The society wars trying by various efforts to raise the tone of these people and do them good. They had gathered over two thousand from the streets and had trained them for domestic service and they were filling these places with credit to themselves. The society had also a large proportion of girls who by reason of physical infirmity were not fitted for domestic service, fie supposed a problem which presented itself to all of them was " What is to become of our crippled children ?" In London alone there were over 6,000 crippled children attending their schools or under school age and as they grew older they would driftinto the world unable to earn their own living. What was to become of them? No one had more cause than himself to be grateful to those ladies who visited cripples in their homes, took them toys and books, organized tea meetings for them, and gave them a fortnight's holiday at the seaside. But when they had done all that they were partically leaving the children in exactly the same condition. To solve this problem the society set to work and by the goodness of God bethought they had solved it. They gathered in gins from the streets and girls who month after month were lying in the midst of suffering and distress and often cruelty; who were drifting to the workhouse or becoming a burden upon charity. These girls were taken in the Society's home where they re- ,see.ined for two years and during that time they 11 ere taught to make artificial flewers such to were on exhibition, that afternoon. T he society had a business method in their work I a nd be held that ell Christian work gla oul d be conducted on business lines. When a

girl could make flowers she was paid so much& dozen or a gross for the work. It cost 3s per week to keep a girl, so that when a girl earned 5s 6d sixpence belonged to her and when she earned 105, 5s belonged to her. It might surprise some of them to know that girls could earn from 10s to 18s per week by flower making. When a girl became a wage earner a spirit of self-dependence was culti- vated, and he thought they would all agree that the highest torm of charity was to help the helpless to a position of self-dependence, Speaking of the excellent flowers made by the girls, the speaker poir6ed out that they were se like French made flowers that many had been sold as such in various shops in th• kingdom and had realized prices three times as large as those originally paid to him (Mr Groom) for them. They ought all to support home indus- tries, and if in doing this they could support such a laudable work be thought it was sufficient to. justify the society coming to Warrington to ask their practical sympathy in the work. The mission I had had no greater sympathisers than the members of the Royal Family. It was his high honour some time ago to represent the Baroness Burdett-Coutts and go to Osborne House and take some of their • flowers for her late Majesty the Queen's acceptance. Teat dear motherly ledy not only accepted the flowers and spoke kindly messages of hope for the prosperity of the work but gave him a large dona- tion towards the support of the mission. The work hat also had untold encourage- ment both from Queen Alexandra and King Ed- ward VII and also from Princess May. In conclusion Mr Groom said that if they had two or three crippled girls in Warrington and did not know what to do with them and their future seemed dark, let him take them to London where he would keep them for two years an reach them to earn their own living. At the end of that time he would bring them back and they would not need a penny of charity and would have no tear of drift- ing and becoming a burden to the rates. This, he said, was the solving of the problem " What shall we do with our crippled children? " and he asked that their sympathy should be extended in the work. (Applause.) At this point a little crippled girl stepped for- ward and handed Mrs Monks a beautiful bouquet of artificial white roses,being a facsimile of the one accepted by :he Princess of Wales wnen her Royal Highness visited the exhibition in London. Mr MONKS said that after the address of Mr Groom it, was not necessary for him to say any- thing to encourage them to patronize the exhibi- tion. One of the best features of the institution was that it helped the children to help themselves and made them indepeudent of charity. It was a real pleasure to help those who could help them- selves. (Applau.se.) The Rev F T RERD proposed a vote of thanks to Mr and Mrs Monks for their presence. le referring to the marvellous expansion of the work he said it all pointed to the fact that a great amount of good might be accomplished in the lifetime of anyane of them. Mr Monks was always ready to give his practical sympathy to anything that teadtd to the well-being of the community. ' Mr Bracirs.m. seconded and sail that Mr Monks was foremo it, in business and in philanthropic work and was never behind in anything to help on a good cause in the good old town of Warrington or else-

here. The vote of thanks was heartily avorded and Mr Monks having replied the buisness of the day com- menced. During the afternoon selections were dis- coursed by Mrs W B Roberts's band. The exhibition was continued on Thursday and Friday and will also be open to-day (Saturday).

THE CRIPPLED GIRLS' WORK.

--- Grand Floral Exhibition in Parr Hall. The interior of Parr Hall presented a scene ot beauty and animation on Wednesday afternoon At first sight a stranger entering the building would no doubt have imagined himself in the midst of a huge horticultural show. Everywhere around were flowers of almost every conceivable variety, of both delicate and brilliant hue, whilst to heighten the charming effect trailing greenery had been introduced in a very tasteful manner. It was only upon minute examination that the fact was revealed that the flowers were not the work of nature but were entirely artificial. This floral exhibition had been organized in order to give Warringtonians an opportunity of extending practical support to a most worthy institution that. The Watercress and Flower Girls' Christian Mission is admittedly one of London's most deserving charities—in fact it is a real God-send to many of the poverty stricken chil- dren, who have been forced to seek a livelihood by t selling flowers and watercress in the 'streets. By means of its cars and influence girls who would in all probability have drifted to ruin have been trained to fill domestic situations and have had cause to feel deeply grateful to those who have taken such an interest in their welfare. The de- partment of the mission with which Warrington is at present more concerned is the Crippled Girls' Industrial Branch and the exhibition which was opened in the Parr Hall on Ti ednes- day afforded ample evidence of the excellent work which is being done in this direction. All the flowers which were on view were the work of crippled girls who have been taken in charge by the Society and trainel to earn their own living. The methods of the mission are fully detailed in the speech by the Superintendent which is reported below. Expressions of admiration of the handi- work of the girls were to be heard on all hands at the opening of the exhibition, and the encomiums which were bestowed were richly merited. As regards the towers, choice chrysanthemums pre- dominated, some splendid prize blooms being on view. The arrangement of the flowers was most picturesque and viewed from the gallery the scene was a very pleasing one. Additional interest was imparted to the exhibition by the appearance of a ontingent of crippled girls from London who with deft fingers were engaged in making flowers. Most of the:productions were on sale,and many were soon disposed, of. There was a large attendance of the public at the opening proceedings, Mr I.l' Monks, Esq., J.P., CC,, who was accompanied by Mrs Monks, presiding. Those present on the platform included Mr J A Groom (superintendent of the Mission), the Revs E C E Carleton, E T Reed, J S Hughes, and W C J Mrs Yonge, Mrs Hughes, and Mr T Birchall. At the outset the Chairman read letters of apology for non-attendance from the Revs I` E Powell and T Bishop, In the course of his letter Mr Powell said :—" The cause for which the floral exhibition is being held is one which must appeal to all who possess the inestimable blessing of soundness of limbs and efficiency of the functions. The sttuggle for existence presses heavily at times even on ethe able-bodied, but how keen and intense must this struggle become to the halt, the lame, and the blind. 1 trust your undertaking will stimulate interest in a class whose very helplessness ought to prove the most eloquent appeal on the pity of the human hearts." Mr J A GROOM, Superintendent of the Institu- tion, then delivered an interesting address. He said the institution that he had the honour to rep- resent owed its existence to the efforts of that truly Christian nobleman, the Earl of Shaftesbury, It was his (the speaker's) lot thirty-seven years ago to lay before his lordship the sad condition of that vast constituency of London's poorest of street poor—the girls and women who were seeking to earn their livelihood by the sale of watercress and flowers in the city--an occupation that necessi- tated them being at the market at four o'clock in

There is a legend amomg Peace Negotiations. the people of Holland that the language spoken in the Garden of Eden was Dutch. English people are as likely to believe this as that the document which arrived at Downing-street late on Saturday night from The Hague is to be an enchiridion of Nevertheless its arrival adds another to the signs that the war is likely to be ended by settle- ment rather than by force. The first of the por- tents was Lord RosEBERY's suggestion at Chester- field of a casual meeting of a diplomatic character brought into relief by F C G's cartoon of the way. side inn and the disguised travellers. Lord Ross- BERY never fights as one beating the air. What 'had been a pious hope with the main body of the Liberal party he put forward as a vision easy of realisation, and it was not too much to assume that he spoke with knowledge of the feeling in Holland, for it was over the Dutch dunes that he looked for the rainbow. After that the public learned of the mysterious visit of the Dutch Prime Minister to London. The joke about his coming to see the Old Masters at the National Gallery having been exhausted, and there being a disposition to take Dr DE KUYPER'S informal action too seriously, it became necessary for both Dawning-street and The Hague to disa- vow the reports being built upon it. This was done so emphatically that for a few days there was a lull in speculation. Dr. DE KUPyER's return to Holland, however, has been followed by a com- munication from the Dutch Government—of which he is PRIME MINISTER—tO Our Govern- ment, and it is BOW under consideration. In a few days it will be published, together with a reply:from our Government, probably written by Lord LANSDOWNE, Whom Dr. DE KUPYER seems to have met in a casual manner while taking the air in Whitehall.

It would be idle to prophesy on the nature of the correspondence, but the presumption is that nothing will be wrought by the exchange of views, however courteously expressed. It would be too much to expect that this effusion in Dutch will act like magic in calming strife. But whatever the result, it will show that the minds of those immediately concerned are inclined to recognise the fatuity of a prolongation of the war. Meanwhile Lord KITCHENER'S steady suc!esses will make the situation clearer. The Boers are receiving fresh proofs every few days that their cause is hopeless, and the British are being re- minded that if it can be avoided this heavy pounding away at an enfeebled foe is a sorry ex- hibition of greatness, and very dear at the price. If these convictions can be only impressed, the road to diplomacy will be easy, although it may not be the ordinary high-road, but more in the nature of a lane. There is no desire on the part of the British people to go on playing a won game with ever-increasing taxation and a rising National Debt. Far-sighted statesmen a:e also moved by other considerations. They know that sentiment has a value which cannot be lightly appraised. If the Boers be induced to lay down their arms voluntarily and to pledge their troth to a settlement which shall give us all we want, they will be more easily welded to the other subjects of the Empire than if they are forced to yield with- out any sort of undertaking on their part,and are • free to plot as they please. The burghers have shown themselves honourable men. Let us get their allegiance to a compact which shall cost us none of the essential conditions for closing the war to our own advantage. Sir HENRY CAMP.. BELL-BANNERMAN has pleaded for this view through evil report and good report, and it would be a sagacious as well as a humane policy to end the war by consent, not by subjugation.

WHATEVER may be Pioneers of Empire. the political differ-

ences aroused by the controversial use of the term "Imperial," there are many oecasions when men of all parties, and of no party at all, must agree in keen satisfaction at deeds which our Empire provokes. Sometimes our feelings nre touched by some martial action—

Educationalists throughout the country will await with interest the introduction of the new Education Bill which was promised in the King's Speech. The intentions of the Government are at present a profound secret, but if Dame Rumour is correct there is every pro'oability that the Government's scheme will grant what the Warrington Corporation has already petitioned Parliament for, viz. : the conferment of School Board powers upon County Boroughs.ln that event it would seem almost useless for the Corporation to proceed with their Bill in the present Session of Parliament. It has been ordered to originate in the House of Lords where it stands little chance of being favourably entertained. The promotion of such a bill will prove costly, especially if a poll is demanded in Warrington, as is promised by the Municipal Non-Trading Committee. Under the circumstances, the Corporation would be well advised in withdrawing the Bill until the educa- tional proposals of the Government are made known. If the Government do not revive their proposal of last year to confer School Board powers on County Boroughs, it will then be time for the Corporation to consider what steps should be taken to meet the demands of the Education Department for additional elementary school accommodation. Some of the oldest Warringtonians do not remember to have seen the roads of Warrington in such a lamentable condition as has been the case during the past few months. The laying of the permanent way for the tramways has been one of the chief causes, but even in thoroughfares which have not experienced the necessary upheaval for tramways the condition of the streets has been disgraceful. The footpaths are covered with slush from the roadways, which, in many cases are simply quagmires—as for instance in Froghan-lane —owing to the macadam being totally cut up by the heavy road traffic. Then the snow which covered the town with its,white mantle on Satur- day was removed from the streets in a very leisurely manner, and there were loud complaints as to the condition of the thoroughfares on Sun- day. The committee responsible ought at least to have seen that the footpaths were brushed or squeegeed. A little more energy in this connection is certainly desirable and necessary.

The best wishes a'large number of people will follow Mr. John Dun, the general manager of Parr's Bank, Limited, in his well-earned retire- ment. His services to the bank were fittingly recognised at the annual meeting of the company in London last Thursday. During the thirty-six years of his connection with the institution he has seen it rise from a purely provincial bank to a national concern of great import- ance. Other banking concerns have been merged into it, and its influenca has been extended year by year until it has attained to its present proud position in the banking world. The commencement of Mr Dun's term of office was concomitant with the bank becoming a limited company. Up to that time the bank had been carried on as a private bank by the late Mr Parr, the founder. As .a limited company, Parr's Bank, Limited, has had an uninterrupted career of suc- cess and much of that success has been due to the energy and business-like qualities which Mr Dun has exercised for such a long term of years. The company has shown its appreciation of his services by granting him £lO,OOO in addition to a retiring allowance—a testimonial in itself of which any bank manager might be proud.
