Possibly the view thE t he took of them may soften criticism against the missionaries whom MARK TWAIN trounced so • severely,) with the result that subscriptions to American missions have gone down with a flop. The missionaries did not come very creditably out of the controversy as to looting, and the argu- ments as to their claim for compensation and the need for making an impression on the Chinese were not effective answers. The best answer is to be found in the fact that the missionaries choose to live—with no prospect of gain or a return to England to enjoy quickly-acquired wealth—among people with whom they have so little in common. "There is something about Pekin," wrote Dr. POOLE, "which sits heavily:on you; a place that lives on a past of four thousand years, that uses the same kind of carts, the same saddles, builds the same houses and temples and tombs, has the same rites and ceremonies as it did a few thousand years ago is not a stimulating place." And life there, of course, is pleasant com- pared with what it is outside the realms of settled government, and among races entirely unculti- vated. Fortunately the trend of modern mission- ary effort is to link the preaching of the gospel with medical work. The Englishwomen who go to the Z3nanas perform a noble duty in this way,and most of the advance guards of the religious organisations now make it part of their work to minister to the body as well as to the soul. It is most important, however, that they should not seek to extend their influence further, and to guide the body politic. It has been said, for example, that a lot of mischief was done in Pekin by the claims to authority which were advanced by the Jesuits, and were construed absurdly enough to be designed to overthrow the Chinese Govern- ment. The old idea of temporal authority still tinges the work of Roman Catholics, though thay may not wish it to do so; and there is need for care in this respect. Rut, in praising the martial deeds of soldiers, we must not forget the self- sacrificing work of those pioneers of Empire—the doctors and clergy --who have no personal ambi- tion to serve.

the concentration of nerve for some rare and ap- parently hopeless deed of heroism, or a long-drawn endurance of conflict in the field against a superior foe, under circumstances which might well cause the courage to fail. And sometimes the whole career seems a sacrifice—a bright ex • ample to correct the tendency to love of ease and vi s inertto which modern civilisations brings in its train. The Outlook, gives a touching obituary notice of one of the noble workers for our country at the outposts of duty—Dß WORDS WORTH POOLE —who has just died of typhoid in Pekin. This in- trepid medical officer was only thirty-three years of age; the value of a life is not counted by its length, but by its texture. Dr POOLE was physician to the forces during General LUGA.RD'S Niger ex- pedition, disarming prejudice against the British entertained by the moo, and soon afterwards ho did good work in British Central Africa. There he contracted so severe a fever that after a leave spent mainly at home, it was thought unsafe for him to return to Africa, and so he became surgeon to the British troops at Pekin. It is related that after the vicissitudes of life in Africa he found existence at the Legation dull, and he pined for a greater call on his energy. It came a few months later in the siege of the Legations, and the warmest tributes of praise were given to the young doctor for his devotion to the sick and wounded. Delicate as he was, he never :spared himself, and, needless to say, after the excitement he fell ill again. He recovered, but his system was undermined, and no one is surprised that he fell a victim to typhoid in the insanitary capital of China. The pen-portrait of the man comes up to what one would expect: Brilliant and brave and kind, particularly attrac- tive both!in manner and appearance, with fresh fair complexion, and blue eyes, of that rare child- blue so seldom seen in adults, his personality, physical and mental, was full of charm and dis- tinction." Such men are the true Empire-builders, and go far to counteract the injury done to British reputation by financiers who endeavour to 'exploit native ignorance :of Western methods and , then are indignant when the people show signs of

1

The mighty hunter of big game has compara- tively few opportunities of showing his prowess in these days, for the reason that big game is be- coming rapidly a thing or the past; and it is only in the wildest and most inaccessible parts of the world that sportsmen can hope for the excitement of an encounter with any beast of man-destro jing propensities. Colonel Sir James Willcocks, of Ashanti fame, who is now on his way to South Africa, has published in the" Empire Review "a very graphic account of his shooting experiences, entitled Reminiscences of Sport in West Africa." He points out that in few parts of the world are there to be found greater opportunities for shooting every kind of game. In the regions of the Upper Niger there are desolate wilds where the foot of man has never trod, and these and the forests, and the swamps of the hinterland abound in wild life. The most excit- ing sport to be had is afforded by the elephant, who fights fiercely in his almost last remaining stronghold on the African continent. Sir James says that the chief obstacle to sport in that coun- try is the difficulty of transport and food supplies, but endless possibilities exist for the sportsmen who wait for the opening up of our newly- acquired territory. Fortunately, the development of the country will not mean the rapid extinction of game. Sir James Willcocks says of its High Commissioner Sir Frederick Lugard, that " he is not only an Empire-builder but a mighty hunter who has shot everything worth shooting in Asia, and throughout the whole African continent. He has no intention of allowing the extinction of elephants on any other beasts for purposes of trade in ivory, &c. ; and if he can only be induced to remain a few years longer it may be taken for certain that he will establish such shooting and game laws as will prevent their extermination."

Writers in the reviews are already beginning to discuss the interesting question of the number of new peerages likely to be created at the time of the Coronation. This act of the Sovereign in raising in rank some of his most prominent sub- jects at the time his rule is confirmed is, of course, as old as sovereignty itself. It had, naturally,.a greater meaning in those ancient kingdoms in which the succession to the Throne was determined wholly or partly by election ; but the practice of the King exercising, as it were, his prerogs.- tive as the fountain of honour in favour of some of those who were acclaiming his rule continued after hereditary right had been 'firmly established, and in our history was as much exercised in the early part of last century as at any time. It is true that up to the fifteenth century knighthood was the honour chiefly conferred by English kings at their coronation; but there was than no settled aristo- cracy comparable to the peerage. and the grant of spurs still had a romantic splendour which made it honourable in the highest degree. At one time the gift of peerages at the coronation seemed to be growing obsolete; but the return of the Stuarts,

MATTERS OF MOMENT• --- A Provincial Letter.

Provincial cyclists, whose other name is legion, must feel particularly interested in the statement which Bir H Vincent had to make in the House of Commons on Wednesday. It had been the inten- tion of the loon member to introduce a bill designed to redress the grievances under which cyclists de- clare they suffer at the hands of railway companies, who are now said to have intimated their willing- ness to come to terms. The proposed measure will consequently not be submitted. and all will agree , probably, that it is much better to have the differ- ences which had arisen composed by mutual con- ciliation than that recourse should be had to an Act of Parliament which the railway companies might have come to regard as oppressive and inimical to their interests. The cyclists have of late been pro- testing vigorously that their demands for a revision of the regulations as to the carrying of cycles by rail, were reasonable and such as could not inflict any loss or serious embarrassment on the carrying companies, whose position appears hitherto to have been dictated by an inability to recognise the fact that the cycle is the engine of a recreation,and something more, that has come to stay.

There is no desire 133 far as this letter is con- cerned to " dabble in politics," but provincials generally may feel disposed to answer the question in their own way, whether it is not a little amaz- ing that Mr Joseph Chamberlain should have chosen at this juncture to tell Europe and South Africa that the election of Mr Runciman, the Liberal can- didate for the representation of Dewsbury, would mean a vote for the Boers ? Suffice it to say that, Mr Runciman, known as a " Roseberyite," was elected on Tuesday.

--- Arrangdments in connection with the forthcom- ing Simultaneous Mission of the Sunday School Union are now in progress in all parts of the pro- vinces, and fairly well advanced. Adults of both sexes, everywhere, should see the importance of these great gatherings, and give them all the sup- port and encouragement that lies in their power. In the larger towns and cities districts are being formed for the proper carrying out of the objects in view, and special services provided for ; so that from the let to the 10'n of February all the schools connected with the Free Churches will be holding special meetings of an evangelistic character. Tne ministers and principal workers in connection with the free churches have in most places organised, and will,of course, take part in the meetings,which it is desired to make as numerous and as popular as possible. The first of the series will, indeed, be held to-day.

It was urgel a week or two ago that the Sugar Trade was committing a tactical blunder in taking precautions so early in the Parliamentary Session as it did against an increase in the duty on that commodity. At first the Chancellor of the Exchequer refused to be drawn" in regard to his intentions by an apparently simple question, but the agitation for the repeal of the tax went on as before, and now Sir Michael Hicks-Beach tells the would-be repealers that he has not received any " facts" as to the inquiry caused by its imposition, adding that " vague and general complaints proved nothing." All that the agitators have hitherto succeeded in doing goes to show that it is some- times prudent to "leave well alone." It may be remarked in this connection that experiments in the growing of sugar beet-roots on the Liverpool Corporation sewage farms at Walton are said to show most satisfactory results, and to encourage the belief that British agriculturists may yet enter into this industry with profit, Dr Sigmund-Stein has reported that the percentage of sugar in the sugar beet-roots grown was is againsb 14.1 per cent. in German roots cultivated in the vicinity of Berlin; that the purity was 8 per cent., against 82 in the German-grown roots; and the weight of the roots per acre 41 tons at Walton, against ten tons in Germany.

Though the language of Mr Abraham, 31. P, as president of the South Wales Miners' Federation, vr.hat vague there , at this week's conference, in dealing with the Stop- nay Policy of the men was some was that in it which led to the belief that he is not quite at one with his colleagues on this subject. "Mabon " has but lately returned from his tour in America, and believes now that if he had been at home such a policy would at least not have been adopted to the extent it was, and that on the other hand, the employers would not have pressed the

!prosecution of the miners' leaders in the way they have done. Be this as it may, Mr Abraham evi- dently fears that the friction which will necessarily follow an action at law may be detrimental to peace in the future, and hopes that difficulties that have arisen may be settled out of court. It has already been shown how determined the leaders of tee men were to make a display of their power a few weeks since, when, after proceedings were commenced, they again ordered the miners to cease work and indulge in "play " with a view to keeping up their wages! It would no doubt tend to the healing of the wounds alieady caused if" Mabou's " hopes can be realised. But even colliery proprietors are only human, and if the general public were asked for an expression of feeling in the matter, it would probably be that the men and their leaders are . scarcely to be trusted, and that a proper laying down of the law bearing upon "Stop-days" is much to be desired.

To Ask Enough," with a view to obtaining what would really satisfy, is a bit of worldly wis- dom of wbich at different times we have many and varied illustrations. In other words, the oracle does pot always answer expectations. Kruger tried it, and what is the result? " Mabon" has this week asked for sixty, seventy, or more Labour representatives in Parliament; and no doubt wishes he may get them. The other day, as noticed in this letter at the, time, a conference in Lonthin of co- operative and trades-union delegates cheerfully passed resolutions declaring that no scheme of Old Age Pensions would be deemed satisfactory that was not universal in its application, and abso- lutely independent of character and thrift on the part of future recipients. Mr. Chamberlain, who is supposed to have some knowledge of the subject' in reply to an Accrington correspondent, says the results of the conference referred to are "distincitly hurtful to the cause." He is convinced that no scheme of universal pensions is ever likely to be accepted, and that the views expressed in that direction tire not calculated to make converts to the principle. Toes() people ask too much, and thereby hinder and injure their chances of getting even what would "really satisfy," by keepingaway from them, or driving off those who might, other- wise be of substantial assistance to their cause.

" Why do you work hard ? " was the insinuating headline of a recent circular said to have been ex— tensively distributed, • through the post, among young gentlemen at Eton and other schools. It re- lated to betting and horse-racing. No wonder that Betting and Gambling should be rampant as they , are with all theee agencies at work. But the Bis- ' hop of Hereford has propos id with a view to plac- ing some check upon such tendencies, that the , Select Committee appointed last Session of Parlia- ment shall be re-appointed. It is expected that the committee will, of course, collect a mass of evi- dence to show that the evils alluded to are on the increase, but of what use the evidence will be to the Legislature is not quite so clear. The public are sometimes reminded that betting and gambling are not entirely non-existent even in religious circles and that occasionally they are serviceable in the , work of raising building and other funds. Not very long since, a circular went around from a Church society protesting against bazaars and some of the little arts practised thereat, and threatening to withhold financial support from these churches in aid of which these vanities were encouraged. The question,therefore,arises whether these would come within the inquiry held by the Select Committee which, it is assumed, has by this time been re-appointed. .The evils are real and need to be dealt with, but what of the remedies ?

RUNCORN-- NOTES.

[By A COIuLESPONDENT.I It is pleasing to note that the finances of the Liberal Club are in a very satisfactory condition though at first sight this does not appear to be so. This year the committee have had to meet some extraordinary expenditure which will not be en- countered during the cominp year so that the de- 1 ficit will be slowly but steadily wiped off. It is also pleasing to see that the Liberals of Runcorn are determined to unite together as in former days. The probability is that Sir John Brunner, will be asked to yearly visit his constituents and thus infuse new vigour and life into the electors of the Northwich Division. Those who remember the po3r organization at the last election will agree that something should be done from a political pint of view in ease an elec- tion should be precipitated unexpectedly. It is none too early to plan out an organization which will stand us in good stead at election times.

According to a resolution nalved by Mr Posnert Runcorn, like its member, is going to follow the fortunes of Lord Rosebery.

There was but little business at the School Board meeting held on Tuesday. Thera was, however, an important pronouncement by Mr Handley. The Board think that the lack of accommodation at the Victoria-road Board School can be amply met by transferring the lower classes to Granville-street, It was pointed out that it was impossible to accommodate all the scholars at the Victoria Schools who are on the registers,which is a lament- able state of things. The transferring ot the scholar would mean an increased grant and a saving to the ratepayers' pockets, which is a consummation devoutly to be wished.

FOOTBALL.

Saturday's was a remarkably good performance and is noteworthy because it is the first time the team have won away from home since November 9th. It would seem ta show that the men are re- turning to their best form, In the packed scrim- mages, the Cheshire lals had not a ten to one chance of :pushing the burly Yorkshiremen, but they made up for this lack of strength by extra. smartness and gained possession with gratifying frequency. And I am pleased to hear the backs executed some fine passing with which Hunslet were unable to cope. The youngsters did well Flannagan's try was a very clever individual effort, and Butterworth's display was equal to his reputa- tion, whichlhas not been the case for some months past, and Moran didicapitally. Young Faulkner was again a success at half back. Walker was the best forward on the field and Wilkinson played splendidly at full back.

For the match with Oidha.n to-morrow Houghton has signified his intention to turn out, his leg now having completely recovered. The full team is ; Houghton, back ; Moran, But.terworth, Warder and Flannagan, three-quarters; Jolley and Richardson, half backs; Walker, Langley, Lightfoot, Woods, Hayter, Moulton, Brooks atm Tomlinson.

The Club have this week signed on a new player in Harry Price, of Runcorn Recreation, This is a very promising player. He is a lengthy youth' with plenty of bone and muscle, and he has done goad service for the Rees at three-quarter. He is fairly fast, passes well. and has a mighty kick for a lad. He is to travel to Oldham with the "A" team to-day.

The Club have had notice to quit their field with the alternative of purchasing. The figure asked is preposterous. Negotiations are proceeding with the solicitors for the owners, and it is probable that the affair will shortly be satisfactorily arranged,

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

PERSONAL.

At tha annual meeting ot the Runcorn Free Church Council on Friday evening, Mr James Pritchard was unanimously ,elecsed president tor the ensuing year. S.IIIPPING ACCIDENT AT RUNCORN. _

On Monday morning, a disquieting rumour had got abroad to the effect that ths flat " Pilot," be- longing to Messrs R Abel a'nd Sons, carriers, Run- corn, had gone down in the Mersey on Sunday with four hands, who belong to Runcorn. The flat went down the riser to collect ft cargo of sand, and it was reported that in the rough weather on Sunday, the boat had been sunk. On inquiries on Monday however it transpired that the flat which had been fully exposed to the fury of the gale, had been run ashore tor safety,and it floated with Monday's noon tide, and was able to proceed up the Mersey with a small cargo. SAD FATALITY AT RI:INCA/N.

On Wedn—esday an inquest was held at the Run- corn Police Court touching the death of a sailor named Bernard Caffery whose body was recovered on Tuesday from the Ship Canal.—The evidence showed that on Tuesday night the deceased was going from his ship to buy some food.when he must have fallen into the water. A boy, named Baden, heard the cries for assistance but by the time he summoned help the sailor had disappeared. I He was afterwards grappled for in the Slap Canal ,1 and when found was quite dead.—A verdict of " Found drowned" was returned.

DESERT/NG HIS WIFE. On Thursday at the Runcorn Police Court Henry Wyche was charged with deserting his wife at Stockham whereby she had become chargeable to the common fund of the Runcorn Union.—The evidence showed that the woman had been charge- able to the Union since the 6th August and all efforts to find him had been fruit- less, Mr Cooke relieving officer discovered however, in an evening paper that a man of prisoner's name bad been summoned at Northwich and inquiry showed that he was the man wanted. It was stated that prisoner had previously suffered inprisonment for refusing to contribute to the maintenance of his parents some years ago.— Prisoner was committed to Knutsford for two months, CYCLE ACCIDENT IN CHURCH-STREET.

On Friday afternoon two lady cyclists were passing along Church-street when Mrs Hall, an elderly woman, apparently unconscious of their approach, attempted to cross the thoroughfare. One of the lady wheelers just managed to get clear of her, but the other cyclist collided with the woman, knocking her down. Mrs. .Hall sustained a nasty gash on her head which bled profusely, and she was rendered unconscious. She was picked up by Messrs 0 Watson and T Briscoe,and carried into her home at the rear of Mr. Bankes', hairdresser. Here a doctor was summoned with all possible haste, but such was the shock sustained that she did not recover consciousness until Sunday morn- ' ing. It seems that the unfortunate woman is some- what deaf, and that may explain why she did not hear the cyclists approaching. She is now making satisfactory progress towards recovery. BOARD SSHOOL TEACHERS ENTERTAINED BY MR AND Mats. NORMAN.

Last Friday evening all the teachers employed by the Runcorn School Board assembled in Bruns- wick School, in response to an invitation from Mr and Mrs F J Norman. During the evening choice fruit was served round, and each one was presented with a ill). paeket of tea.

WARRINGTON AND THE

CORONATION.

IHow must Warrington celebrate the Coronation of the King and Queen? That is a subject which is already engaging the attention of the members lof the Town Council. The matter was referred to at the meeting of the General Purposes Committee held at the Town Hall on Monday evening- The Mayor, Mr J C Parr, presided and in the course of a brief speech, he expressed the earnest hope teat Warrington would not be behind other towns in its manifestations of loyalty on that important occa- sion. This expression of opinion was heartily as- sented to aria it was ultimately agreed that the Par- liamentary Committee should take into considera- tion the best method of celebrating the Coronation of the King and Queen. The Coronation will take place on Thursday, June 26th. There is already a consensus of opinion in the town that the usual holidays connected with Walking Day should be held in the same week, and thus prevent a couple or breakages in different weeks. There seems little difficulty in the way to prevent the holding of Walking Day on the day following the King's , Coronation, and if this coarse is adopted, the customary " trip •' day will follow on the Satui day. Nothing definite has been decided upon as to the best method of celebrating the Coronation, although suggestions have been informally made. One of them is a grand, procession of , trades and friendly societies, on a somewhat similar scale to that witnessed on the occasion of the jubilee of the late green's reign in 1837., A grand firework display has also been mentioned but the feeling is gaining ground that something of a permanent character would be preferable Warrington has.nothing of a permanent character to remind its citizens of previous national celebra- tions and it is felt that a fitting memorial to the late Qreen should form a part of the impending celebrations. A public subscription list will be issued at an early date.

The Secretary reported that the work during the past year had been one of consolidation and was, a fairly satisfactory one. Additional expenses had been found necessary over and above those at first con'emplated set after the payment of these out of the yeas' revenue the balance for the year was slightly more favourable than at the beginning. The nett income had been £159 193 and the expenditure £153 181 91. These RUMS excluded the balance in the hands of the Secretary and the amount owing on capital account. Sir John Brunner had kindly offered to forego the inter- est and repayment of the amount expended in recon- struction until the prior liability bad been discharged. As good use had beea made of the reading room during the year the committee to make it mare useful had ex- pended a greater sum on literature than:in the previous year. The committee's thanks were duo to all who had given and lent magazines, etc. The lecture room of the club gave excellent facilities for the Progressive candidates at the recent Board School election and alaia proved the necessity of the enhvgement and reconstruction of the club. The committee would ever keep in view toe education of their members in Liberal pricciples of which the club would be a rallying place for discussion, etc. The secretary, in reply to Mr Norman pointed out that a great deal of extraordinary expenditure had been met this year which would not have to be met again, There was every prospect that the overdraft at Parr's Bank would be thus steadily diminished. The report was approved. THE APPOINTMENTS. VACCINATION ORDERS.

A circular WB9 received from the Local Government Board drawing attention to the fact that in face of the outbreak of smallpox they lead decidcd not to limit the supply of calf lymph to primary vaccination only but for re-vaccination also. They were now issuing to public vaccinators a quantity of lymph very much in excess of the normal supply to meet the additional demand. _ _ _

_ The Clerk said that he had forwarded the letter to the public vaccinator. THE ISOLATION OP-SMALLPDX PATIENTS.

A circular on the name subject was also received from the Local Government Board drawing attention to the considerable risk of small pox being spread by moans of casual paupers and trusted that the Guardians would take measures to diminish the danger. Only circum- stances of grave urgency justified the admission or retention of a small pox csse in a workhruse, and measures, in such a case, should Le taken to have all the inmates and persons employed to attend on the patient, vaccinated. The Clerk stated that he had forwarded the copy of the circular to the Medical Officer of the workhouse:

VISIT OF DELEGATES re CONSUMPTIVE PATIENTS.

Mr A R Norman, who with Mr Moseley Williams, was appointed a delegate to attend a conference dealing 1 with the question of conaumptive Inmates, gave a de- tailed account of the conference. Be said that he fourd out of the five unions represented that most of the dele- gates went there, like themselves, for information. They were unanimously of opinion taat the treatment of consumptives was as yet in its infancy and that no expense should be incurred until they knew bow the treatment would turn out. Some were enthusiastic, and were disposed to petition the County Council to take up the matter. Others thought that unions could run a sanatorium of their own. But the ultimate decision was that the conference requested the County Council to consider the advisability of provid- ing a sanatorium for the treatment of consumptives. It was thought that it would b 3 better to pay two or three sums of 17s 6d for consumptives rather than establish a sanatorium of their own. It wouri,be inadvisable to spend money until something had been proved. Thu Master of the house, in reply to the Chairman, said that there was only one consumptive case in the 'house at present and that there had not been a con- sumptive in the house before. The Chairman said it would be satisfactory for ths public to 'know that. PUBLIC VACCINATORS'FEES.

The Clerk submitted a comparative statement of vaccination fees paid to public vaccinators. Tho total fees for 1898,were £5B es 4d as compared with £289 15s for 1901. The Chairman said that the figures showed a great increase. Mr Reynolds stated that a doctor speaking to him a chart time ago said that he would like to revert to the old system. From a financial point of view it would pay him much better. The Clerk reported the dnath of Miss Emily Douglas (which took place on Sunday the 19th inst) an ass6tant teacher at Mill Brow School:--4 letter was read from Mr T C Haddock resigning his position as treasurer to the Board in consequence of ill health and it was recom- mended that his resignation be accepted and that he be thanked for his past services. It was reported that £/33 53 was to amount of the fee grants and cleat the overseers had had paid into their accounts the sum of £5OO. THE OVEEtCROWDINO AT VICTORIA BOARD SCHOOL. Mr Handley moved the following resolution. "As the monthly returns of the school a:tend ances for some time past show overcrowding in Victoria-road Schools, the Clerk to prepare a statement and report at the next meeting of the School Management Committee as to the practicability of transferring a sufficient numberl of scholars cf the lower standards, to reduce the overcrowding, from the Victoria-road to the Granville-street school." Mr Handley,in the course of • lengthy and detailed speech showed that there were more scholars on the registers of the Victoria-road school than it was possible to find room for. If they all presented themselves on a given day the teachers would be compelled to refuse a great number admit- tance. It would be much better, he argued, to remove the lower classes to Granville-streot in order to make way for the upper scholars. If they were to use the surplus accommodatisn in this way at Granville-street, they Could get more scholars, earn a larger grant and thus save the pockets of the ratepayers. Canon Wood seconded and the proposition was passed. ANNUAL REPORT OF THE SCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMUITEE. The annual report submitted showed an increase of 61. in the average number oo the school register com- pared with Hie previous year ; an increase of 19 in the average uttendance ; parents summoned betore the committee, 779, an increase of 2 ; prosecutions before the magistrates 171, a decrease of 39. A total of 305 children of school age left Runcorn 1900-1901. The number of canal boat children 98. It was reported that the percentage of attendances is 85.98 (including boat children) exceeding that of hest year, which was the highest previously recorded. The severe epidemic of measles seriously affected the attendance in all departments of the schools, it extended from the early part of October to Christmas, 1900, and necessitated the closing of all the Infants' School in turn for part of this period. The increased average attendance however had earned in Government Grants about Rue 103 more than last year, which was £6l 10s. in excess of the previous one. It was especially gratifying to note the improved attendance of boat children as shewn by the following percentages of attendance for corresponding nerieee, viz :-1819. percentage 25 7. 1900, percentage 29.8. 1901, percentage 34.8. The new bye-laws became operative in Islay last, but the Monthly returns shew the increased penalty for non-3ttendance had not secured the results anticipated,the magistrates in four cases only having partially exercised their pgwers. This was probably owing to the belief that some parents are still ignorant of the new conditions. The average number of children absent every time the schools were opened was 488, resultieg In a loss in Government Grants to the Schools in Runcorn, after making full allowance for r excusable causes, of about £531 103. Parental neglect by practically the same persons was the cause of this great evil. Father Chambers, as chairman, in moving the adop- tion of the report, said they could congratulate them- selves on the fact that there was a decrease in the prosecution before the magistrates compared with previous years. There should be, he thought, special legislation for boat children. It was unfair that Runcorn should be saddled with so many boat children. The Clerk stated that if they excluded the boat children from the attendance there would be a percentage of 88, which would be an exceptionally good One. This concluded the business.

OPENING OF A VILLAGE HALL AT

HALTON. The Gift of Sir John Brunner, Bart., M.P.

Sir John T Brunner, Bart, M.P., formally opened on Saturday a village ball which he has generously pro- vided for the free use of the parishioners of Halton. The building, formerly known as the Primitive Method- ist Chapel, was recently purchased by Sir John, and he has borne the cost of putting it into a thorough state of repair, adapting it for tne meetings of the Parish Council, Iced frienoly societies, educational,social, and recreative gatherings and the like. By means of a fold- ing screen the hall can be divided into two portions, so that it will be possible for two meeting to be held on the premises simultaneously. The furniture is also in keeping with this idea, bolding forms being pro- vided which can be put into. a very small space when not required for use. On Saturday prior to the opening proceedings, there was ga special meeting ot the Parish Council, it which the following resolution was unanimously adopted, on the motipn of Mr W Moores (ctairman), second-d by Mr Jeacock :—" Tba-, this Council tenders its most sincere and hearty thanks to Sir John Brunner, Bart, for the kind and generous offer made this atternoon to tue parish of Hatton (through trio medium of the Parish Council) of the free use of the Parish Hall for any pur- pose which would tend to promote the general good o the parishioners or any pert of them,and that this Conn cil pledges itself to use every means in its power to , further and encourage the proper use of the ball " Sir John . was subsequently entertained to tea by the Council. Besides the bon baronet there were ' present G D Wray (vicsr),R*v S Willeinacn(vicar of Timperiey), Rev 11 Morrison (resident Wesleyan minister), Mi John Ockleston (representative on the County Council), Mr Thomas Wright (district coun- cillor), Mr W Handley, Mr John H Gamon, Mr Ii Vi Handley, the members of the Parish Council, viz : Messrs W Moores (chairman), J Asbury, S Barrow, A Fryer, John B Houghton, R Hughes, G Rulate, John S Jeacock, J Looker, J Manning, W Moores, A Paves', Joseph Stokes,and A Woodier (clerk). After the tea the ball was opened to the public and very few minutes elapsed before it was filled with an enthusiastic audi- ence.

In addressing a crowded meeting presided over hope that by the Rev G D Wray,'the Parish Council would transact their business in the vicar of :the parish, Sir John expressed the builcling,and that the inhabitants generally would make use of it for their amusement, recreation, and improve- ment, and that it would become the seat of a public library. It was an allegation against parish councils that they easily occupied themselves in doing nothing. He was not one of those who depreciated the work of putting a plank across a ditch. It might be at immense importance to a considerable number of people. There were many public rights, especi- ally in the rural neighbourhoods, which were of great value, of great convenience, and a source of great pleasure to people. For the sake of the present and of future generations the ratepayers ought to stand up as men for their rights and not allow one to be taken away. The duty of protecting public rights was thrown upon parish councils, and he hoped t'ao Halton Council would discharge that duty fearlessly. It would ,be their duty to maintain that village hall. Sir John commented strongly upon the fact that while parish Councils could receive gilts town councils and district couecils could not do so without the august and gracious permission of the ',mid G avernm^nt Board, obtained in a manner that was an idiotic waste of pub- ho time. The Leaislaturo had got into such a pitiable mess that no member of the House of Commons could hope to get a bill passed to fill up this hiatus in the law, and he had suggested that the House of Lords, which had nothing on earth to do just cow, should pass the necessary bill. Here was Coronation year, and prob- ably many wealth 7 men and wealthy women might be moved by public spirit to celebrate the coronation of King Edward VII. by gifts, and if every authority had the right to receive gifts many more gifts would be made and many men like himself and a 'arc(' number oficommunities like Haltoa would have mutual pleasure: Mr J Stokes proposed, and Mr T Wright seconded a vote of taanks to Sir John, to which he briefly responded.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES

A SHOCKING CASE OF YOUTHFUL DEPRIVITY.

At the Warrington Police Court on Saturday, before Mr Direhall and other magistrate., Henry Beckett,a young man, was charged with keeping a brothel at 37a.lborne-street, during the months of December and January, and Ellen Travis and Mary Taylor, eget nineteen and seventeen re- spectively, were charged with aiding and abetting him.—At the outset the Chief Constable said he did not purpose to offer any evidence against the girls. ant he would ask the magistrates to discharge them in order that he might call them as witnesses. He was satisfied that the girls were more . .

to be pitied than punished, and were more alined against than sinning.—The magistrates agreed to this course and the girls were dlscharged.—Dealing with the charge against the man, the Chief C ,nstable said , the facts disclosed a state of things which were alike monstrous and deplorable. It appeared that the prisoner, who was n)t twenty-one years of age and was a single man, was until recently a cab-driver, but about six weeks ago be took it into his head to go to Mr Smith. estate agent, ILegh-street. and take • cottaee In Golborne-street He put into the house some miserable apologies for articles of furniture and then appeared to have cast about for some poor unfortunate female victims for the purpose of earning his miserable subsistence. He got bold of the girl Travis first. She was employed in a situation In Wilderepool-road, and was :induced by the prisoner to go to the house in Golborne-street. By persuasion and the aid of some drink he contrived to make her stay there all night with the result that she was dischargei from her situation and continued to live with him. In a similar manner he got hold of the girl Taylor. The Chief Constable proceeded to describe the subsequent operations of the prisoner, the details being of a revolting character —Prisoner , was then charged with assaulting a woman named Susannah I Hughston at 5 o'clock the previous Saturday morning.— Bvidence was given by Mrs Hug hston and the girl Travis to the effe3t that complainant went to the house for her husband arid was immediately set upon by the prisoner who thumped her and banged her against the wall —The Chief Constable said that In the course of his twenty-two years experience he had dealt with many cases of a similar nature, but with none so bad as the present one.—The magistrates sentenced the prisoner to three months imprisonment with hard labour for the first offence and a month for the assault. The Chair- man said the Bench wished to call the attention of people who had houses of this description to let They hoped that the agents would make inquiries as to the character of those who wished to become tenants and see that the houses did not come into the hands of the such disreputable people as In the present case. Mr Taylor. P.C.M., promised to take steps with regard to the future career of the girls, the Chair- man suggesting that they should be sent to a home. A FATHER PROSECUTES 11Is DAUGHTER.—NO FELONIOUS At Daresbury Petty Sessions oa Tueeday,Muriel Wilkinson. aged sixteen, of Whitler, was charged on remand with having stolen a mare, a ladles' gold watch and chain, and sundry articles of clothing, the property of Daniel Wilkinson on the 22nd of January. She pleaded guilty.—The father stated that his daughter had been acting as housekeeper for ant on the day named he left home at 830 to go to his work, leaving her in charge of the house. On returning at eleven o'clock he found the mare was missing and the "house upside down." Information was given to the police. He would not have had his daughter brought to the court had he known it was she who had taken the erticles.—P C Need- ham stated 'hat at twelve o'clock on Wednesday morning he received information of the robbery and proceeded to Frodsham. wisere be met the defendant. She said she was going horns with the mare.—Defendant said her mother told her that the things were to be divided amongst them. She was under the impression that her mother had given her the things before she died, with the exception of the mare.—The Magistrates' clerk said there was no felonious intent, and the magistrates dismissed the case.

Scholars' Tea-meetings at Wycliffe. On Monday evening,the elder scholars of Wycliffe Sunday school enjoyed their annual tea meeting. IThere was an excellent attendance of scholars. The teachers of the various classes presided at the tables. Alter tea the entertainment (which was provided by the scholars and teachers) was thrown open to parents and friends. The Hey John Yonge (pastor) occupied the chair, and distributed the prizes for regular attendance, A very pretty con- tribution to the entertainment was a song and dance entitled "Pinafore," which was gracefully rendered by about a dozen little girls. The boys performed their part well in a dialogue entitled "Dr Spanker's School." Tole was an amusing sketch, for in less than ten minutes they had gone through a song, a dance, arid a pillow fight. An amusing stump speech was contributed by Mr Harry Hill. Miss Alice Be3wick cleverly recited " The way to heaven." Other recitations were given by same of the boys and girls. Miss Jennie krmstong sang "Only tired." Miss Annie Wallington ably accom- panied teiroughout the evening's proceedings. The programme wound up with a dialogue entitled " A nice quiet morning," which was undertaken by six young men. The sketch caused much merriment and was very well performed,—On Tuesday even- ing about 120 infant scholars had their tea. An entertainment was afterwards held at which the children sang and recitel. The Rev and Mrs Tonga presented their customary prizes to the best boy and girl. recinrs. Mr Turbytiell (of London) also gave an ex:ra priz 3. Books were givan to all boys and girls who recited and each child received an illuminated text card and au orange at the close of the proceedings.

SINGULAR FAMILY DISPUTE. ---- A Sankey Farmer Sued in the County Court.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday week, before his Honour Judge Bowen Rowlands,K.C,an action was heard in which ,f,mirnt Millard, Peel-rose, Eccles, sued Matthew Pownall,farmer,Grear Sankey,for £l4 Is, the claim being made up as follows i—Money lent to defendant:3 wife, £ll 15 ; for services rendered to the defendant £1 ; board and lodgings in the 13Ie of Man whilst renderieg these services £1 63.-11 r McKeever, barrister (tusructed by 11,e55rs Walker D3an an! Com- eau), Manchester) appeared for the plaiatiff and defea- darn W•tel represented by Mr A Browne. Mr McKeever said the plaintiff was ,a widow and her daughter was the wife of the defe.adant who was a farmer in a very good p,sition, and in October last de- tendant's wife being in bad health she was sent to tha Isle of Man to recuperate accompanied by a little girl as a companion, and whilst Mrs Pownell was in the Isle of man, owing to the Demean Bank failure, a I large nu.nber of sAes were going on and Mrs Pownall purchased a quantity of articles. About October 10h, Mrs Pownall wrote to her mother asking her to send her £lO, as she wished to buy a necklace. Plaintiff wrote advising her not to buy it, and shortly after wards Mrs Pownall wrote another letter asking for a remittance, and in consequence plaintiff drew £lO from the bank and sent it to her daughter. Subsequently plaintiff went over to the Isle of Man and found her ' daughter was entirely without money. Ka° lent her £1 153, and eventually defendant came over and his wife continued to attend the sales. Mr Pawned now appeared to have suspected that his wife's mind was not as it oueht to be and a doctor having been consulted she was taken to Englaedland subsequently sent to an asylum. Mr Pownall instructed the plaintiff to remain at the Isle of Man and settle up certain bills. Plaintiff remained four days and she claimed from the defendant 53 a day for services ren- dered. It appeared that defendant's wife had pur- chased articles to the extent of £6O, including furniture and bedding and the necklace in vestion and these were sent to Mr Pownall who sold all except the neck- lace. Although £lO was sent Co Mrs Pownall to buy the necklace they believed she did not use the money f3r that purpose (having obtained the amount from some other source) but she bought other articles with it, which had been sent to the defendants. It was cmtended that the defendant, having taken possession of the property and sold it, was liable for the money lent for the purpose of purchasing it. The other item of the claim was £1 163, expenses incurred for board and lodging by the plaintiff alter defendant's departure from the Isle of Man when she was settling the bills. Plaintiff gave evidence bearing out this statement. Mr Browne submitted tnat he had no case to answer. No agency on the part of the defendant's wife had been shown or proved. When defendant sent his wife to the Isle of Man he gave her a blank cheque and £lO in cash. It was a deplorable case and had cost the defen- dant a large amount of money. Mrs Pownall had no need for the money lent by the plaintiff. At the sales she bought the most absurd things which when sold realized only £4. His Honour-said he could see no evidence of rati fica tion with regard to the first part of the claim. . _ .

Mr McK iever : Defendant admits he has received the necklace and has it still in_ his nossemion.

Mr Browne We have p +id £l3 for it since. Mr McKeever pointed out that no portion of the £lO was expended in purchasing the necklace. He under- stood it was admitted that the money was applied to the purchase of other things. Mr Browne : No. His Honour : That would not be ratification. There is no evidence that defendant authorized the purchase of the co-AA.

_ His Honour then gave judgment for the defendant in respect of the £lO. Dsaiing with the loan oi 15s Mr Browne submitted lb t Mrs Pownall had no need to aik for the money. Referring to the claim for services rendered and board and lodgings, Mr Browne said the plaintiff volunteered to remain at the Isle of Men and 83ttle the bills. His Honour gave ludgment for the defendant on the whole claim without costs.

" going, and this is my object in giving you this.. " spontaneous recommendation. It possesses in a. This match, postponed from December 21, Was played at Oldham, On Tuesday. Halifax brought their full strength, while the home team was disorganised in the back division by the absence of Lees and Civil, which rendered it necessary to bring in Brady at half book, and Dawson at centre. Thus both the Oldhsm centres, Moore and Dawson, late of Ponty- pridd were new players under Northern Union rules, and the result was that the two speedy wings. Buzzer end Spottlswoode, had no ettective support. Frater started for Oldham against a strong wind,and Rigg scored after a strong run from midfield in the first two minutes. Little adding a goal. THE INFIRMARY BALL.

List of Guests.

At the Lion Hotel, Warrington,on Thursday,week tine annual ball in aid of the funds of the Warrington Infirmar) and Dispensary was held and the function was quite as successful as in previous years. About 180 guests were present, including a party from Appleton Hall. The scene in the ballroom was a brilliant one, the dresses of the ladies being exceedingly tasteful and picturesque. The duties of M.C's were carried out by members of the ball committee. Mr R H Pearson's band was in attendance and played a capital selection of music. .••••••11110...••••••• Concert in the Parish Room, Latchford. The annual scholars' concert and prize distribu- tion was held in the Parish Room, Latchford on Monday evening, when there was a crowded atten- dance, The Vicar, the Rev F A Sereeton, presided, and in his address said that the results in both de- partments of the school were better this year than last, and that the scheol had obtained an excellent merit grant. The school children were arranged at 1 the beck of the platform, and the various choruses were heartily sung. Solos were given by the Misses Lily Welsby, Annie Hamblett, Ann Green, and Gladys Hall, and Masters Harry Marsh, Arthur Holliday, Harold Bradbury, and George Ralph's. The prizes gained during the year were then dis- tributed by Mrs Fairclough to the successful scholars, and a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to her for her services. Part two of the programme consisted of the operetta " Cinderella and the Prince," the various characters being taken by the following :—Cinderella,Valerie Grindley ; fairy god 'mother, Nellie Davies ; Priscilla and Jane (Cinderella's sisters), Lily Walker and Emily Higham ; fairy queen, Lucy Monks. Prince (and disguised as soldier), Wilfred Clark • Baron (Cinde- rella's father), Ernest Banks; herald, John Hopkins The singing and acting was excellent throughout and reflected the greatest credit upon Miss Place and her staff of helpers.

During the hearing of a tradesman's claim for £lO against aworking man, at the Whitby County Court, Judge Raikes said he would like to see it made illegal for a tradesman to allow credit to a working man beyond the amount of his week's wage 6. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. VvyclifFe P S.A. Society.

• Wycliffe oburch was filled with a large congregation on Sunday afternoon on the occasion of the "open meeting "in connection with the P.S.A. Society. Mr Thomas Bitchall, J.P., occupied the church and stated that be was very pleased to see so many present on such a day. He proceeded to speak on the virtue of " Earnestness " and advised his hearers to be earnest about something but to let that something be worth being earnest about. There was a general complaint just now of the sin of indifference; indifference in the church, in educational movements, temperance ques- tions, political organisations and every movement for the elevation of the people. There were people who could be earnest about amusements. He did not decry amusements but he felt there was a danger in over-doing them. It grieved hira to see so much enthusiasm for amnsements and yet there were so few who had any words of righteous indignation against the evils around them. There were young men who had passed through the Sunday schools who could get up an unlimited amount of enthusiasm for cricket and football and yet who could live in our crowded to wns where drunkenness and vice were rampant, where foulness of language and obscenity of every kind were almost becoming the common language of the people, without raising their voices against it. He urged his hearers not to be ashamed to promote a better and a brighter state of things.—Mr J W Reader, of Liverpool, brought greetings from the P.S.A. brotherhoods at Lisc,rd and Seaforth which were reciprocated. He afterwards proceeded to speak on " Sowing and Reap- ing," and by a number of illustrations he showed how men and women reaped bad results from ill-conduct in life. The Warrington Apollo Male Voice Choir con- tributed several glees in an accomplished manner under the conductorship of Mr T Brough. The choicest piece of the afternoon was the " 'The Chapel " or the vesper bell, which was given with commendable feeling and exisression. The service was also taken part in by Mr A Downes, Mr Healey and Mr B Farrar. A collection was taken on behalf of the ft:nris of the society.

PARR'S BANK, LIMITED. The Manager's Retire.nent. A Handsome Gift. The thirty-seventh annual meeting of Parr's Banic.Limited,was held on Thursday last week at the Cannon-street Hotel, London,when Mr Cecil F Parr, chairman of directors, presided over a large gathering of shareholders. The directors present included Sir D ivid Gamble, Barr, C.B . Lord Farquhar, Mr E W Nix, Mr John Dun, and Mr A J Fraser.

The Chairman, in moving the adoption of the re- port and accounts, and toe declaration of a divi- dend of 19 per cent for the half-year, with a bonus of 1 per Cent, said that he would begin his remarks with an apology. It would be noticed in the re- port that the directors announced their intention in future to revert to the custom of paying the dividends half-yearly instead of quarterly. They had the courage some years back to inaugu- rate quarterly dividends, and they had now the courage to come and say that the innovation had proved a distinct inconvenience. The practice had always involved a considerable amount of clerical work, and, in addition, there were other objections. The next dividend after the one to be then declared would be payable in July. A branch of the bank had been opened at Llandudao, and shortly they ;mild open a sub-branch at west Kirby, which was a growing suburb of Birkenhead. As a rule, these smaller branches did not bring much grist to the mill, except after a good many years ; but their establish- ment was partly a protective measure. The board regarded them in a sense as a sort of blockhouse for the better securing of accounts within their sphere of work. He was sorry to say that death had been busy in their ranks, and during the six months passed they regretttd to have bit Mr Emil Heinemann, whose experience made him a most valuable director. They had also to regret the prema- ture death of Mr L Munroe, the assistant manager,who had already made his mark in the banking world. With the exception of the alteration in the board there was nothing in the report to distinguish it from its immedi- ate predecessors. Nevertheless, that meeting marked the close of a distant epoch in the history of Parr's Bank. He alluded to the retirement of his friend, Mr John Dun, from the post of general manager.a poet he had held for thirty-six years, indeed, ever since his (the chairman's) father turned his private bank into a limited company. Tnrough all these years Mr Dun had brought his ability and energy and his most capable mind to the further- aneo of the bank's welfare (applause.) He could not think what Parr's Bank would have been without him. But be (the chairman) did know, as many of the share- holders knew, what be had done for the bank—in fact, he had turned a small bank into a large one. (7,heers.) In confidence, he might tell them that Mr Dun had artistic tastes into the bargain. Unlike many strong men, he had al ways recognised that to invite co-opera- tion from his subordinates was a mark, not of weakness, but of strength, and accordingly, he gave thorn his actual confidence—in a word he not only worked by them and through them, but with them (Applause.) He had been a tower of strength to that bank, and his good-nature and urbanity never failed him, making it a pleasure to work with him, and be (the chairman) both personally and officially thanked him most warmly. He had devolved the management on the capable shoulders of Air R W Whafley. As an earnest of the Board's grati- tude, and to mark their sense of his services, they had voted him, in addition to the retiring allowance, a sum of £lO.OOO. (Applause). In his capacity as director, Mr Dun would continue to give them the bene- fit of his great knowledge of banking in general, and of Parr's Bank in particular (Hear, hear.) Turning to the report, the chairman said the various figures in it differed very slightly in their proportion from those in the balance sheet issued in July last. The deposits had more than well maintained the average over the past six months, and the money lodged showed an increase of £420,000 over that of the previous half-year. They held in cash and short money very nearly half of all their advances, loans and discounts, and held very • nearly one=hird of their liabilities to the public, There had been an increase of 1,281 in the number of accounts, so that they were still progressing. Their gross profits of 2318.000 were only £2,000 less than.a year ago, and only £l,OOO less than last July. When they considered the condition of the money market, it having been very unfavourable to bankers, the shareholders would see that the figures were very satisfactory. The bank was now in a sound and healchN condition, and needed no words from him to commend it to them. He cvncluded by moving the adoption of the report and accounts, and that a half-year' divi- dend be declared at the rate of 19 per cent per annum and a bonus of 1 per cent. Mr Edward WNix seconded the motion. The motion was then put to the meeting, and carried unanimously. On the motion of Me Nix, the following retiring directors were re-elected i—Mr Edward Banbury, Mr Alfred Crewdson, Sir J J Harwood, Mr Robert Neill,Mr James Tullock, and Mr A M Turner. Mr Lawrence moved;and Mr Coles seconded,a resolu- tion of thanks to Mr John Dun, the general manager, for his services extending over a period of thirty-six years' The resolution was married by acclamation. Mr Dun, whe was deeply moved, returned thanks ira a few words. He could not, he said, find words to acknowledge all the flattering remarks made concerning him. He had felt the weight of age, but he hoped for some years to come to be able to discharge the duties of director. (Applause). A vote of thanks to the assistant general manager and the other officials was neat received and passed, and a L suitable reply made to Mr Whalley.

Warrington Caledonian Association and " Burns's Nicht."

The members of this association held their annual " Burns's nicht" supper and concert last Friday night in the Patten Arms Hotel, Thera was a good muster of members. Mr James Mow°, president, occupied the chair, and was supported by Dr. Ferguson (vice-president), Mr Skinner, Mr T May. Mr Ewart, and Mr Lee (secretary.) After supper and the loyal, patriotic, and " Burns's immortal memory" toasts had been enthuiastically honoured, Mr Joseph Hunter opened the musical programme with the song, "There was a lad was born in Kyle." The prozramme throughout was a very enjoyable one, one of the principal features being the contributions, "Annie Laurie" and" Banks and Braes," given by the glee party, Messrs Aukland Davenport, Scattergood, and Whitehouse.

The annual report of the Loyal Order of Ancient Shepherds (Warrington District) has been prepared It is signed by John Haley, Pro CS. Joseph Williams, D Prov CS., James Parr,district treasurer and Peter Edgar, district secretary. They state that they are proud to notice that financially, the lodges without exception have had a most success- ful year. In many instances substantial sums have been added to the capital accounts. This certainly is vary encouraging and the officers earnestly hope that the experience of the past year may be continued. On summarising the accounts, they find that 57 new members have been admitted, while 66 have left and 12 died, leaving the present membership at 922 or a loss of 21. The district funds have reached the handsome figures of £514 lie sid,or a gain of £4l 168 Sid. The accumulated capital of the 010 lodges new ',stands at £4,622 6s 9d as against £4,363 16s 4id land when such respective sums are joined together there is a grand total of £5,136 lla 2id or a total gain of £30073 Id. During the year sick members have received the sum of £566 282 d and en account of death claims the sum of £l9O has been paii. It will be perceived, continues the report, that these latter items are exceedingly favourable when com- pared with the payments for the year 1900. The officers desire to thank thole F members who by their exertions and perseverance have been the means of obtaining 57 new members and also those who have assisted in other ways in promoting the welfare and sumps' of the lodges. They would like to see more of the younger members taking their fair share of responsibility. Members should not allow their enthusiasm for pastimes to over- shadow the duty they owe to the order.

The annual general meeting of the members of this club was held at the Pied Bull Hotel, Newton, on Thurs- day evening. There was a large attendance. Mr R Stone J.P.. presided supported by Messrs J Simpson, W El Hindley, H R Eiindley aad J A Galloway, (secre- taries). The Chairman said as that was the first time they had met together since the opening of the new year, he took that opportunity of wishing the members in- dividually a prosperous new year, and collectively, as members of that club wish them success in the coining season, and that although test season both in cricket and bowling the club came out of it very well indeed, and had occasion to feel proud of what it did achieve, he hoped they would do better this season. (Hear, hear.) The Secretary then read the minutes of the last annual meeting which were confirmed. The balance sheet was read showing the total receipts, including a balance of £5 93 84 from 1900, to be £214 12s 104, and the expendidure £l9l 113, leaving a balance of £23 13 104. The patrons were re-elected. On the motion of Mr J Simpson, Mr R Stone was re- elected president of the club. Mr Stone said he was very much obliged to them for electing him for another year as president of their club. He was afraid he made a very inefficient president but at any rate he had the welfare of the club at heart, and if they were willing Co put up with that inefficiency ' which he was conscious of, be was willing to take the AffiCe and the honour they did him, for another year. (Hear, hear.) Th;Vice-Presidents were re-appointed. Mr W Hindley was re-appointed Hon Treasurer on the motion of the Chairman, who said that the Newton Cricket Club owed very great deal to him in the past, and they all looked forward to the success of the club in the years to come with Mr Hindley as its treasurer. He coc.ld not help feeling that in all clubs the officers who take a leading part in its management were open at times to criticism, but with all that criticism he could not help thinking that the members of the Newton Club felt in their hearts that they were very glad they had Mr Hindley as their treas rer. (Hear, hear.) Mr J A Galloway was re-appointed bowling secre- tary. Mr fl R Hindley last season's cricket secretazy,reti from the position, and Mr Thomas Williams was appointed in his place. Mr W Hindley was re-appointed cricket captain, and Mr James Sweetlove was appointed captain of the second team.

Messrs Bodmer, T Pairclough, G McCormick and El Ho'croft were appointed bowling members. A motion by Mr W H Parks that the committee con- sist et 16 members was defeated, the amendment ny Mr T Fairclough that the committee consibt of 12 as heretofore being easily carried. Tte following were appointed the Committee of Management Messrs J Croutchley (sen), T Wilkinson, R Chandley,tl Hill, J Lennox, J W Croutchley, R Carson, H R Hindley, R Wilkinson, J Rimmer, T Rudd and T Boardman. Meisrs T Mayor and E Leyland were re-elected auaitors.

It was decided to join the Lancashire and Cheshire, and Newton Division Bowling Associations. Mr J. A. Galloway was appointed representative to the Lancashire and Cheshire Bowling Association, and Messrs T Fairclough and J 1/7 Croutehley were appointe delegates to that and the Newton Division Bowling Associations.

Mr F. Hutchins was apoointnd groundsmin.

CONSTIPATION'S RAVAGES. Bile Beans a Certain Remedy.

The Opinion of a Great Physician.

That celebrated physician, the late Sir Andrew Clark, expressed the firm 'opinion that constipation was the cause of one half of the ailments from which women and girls suffer. It fills the blood with impurities, robs the system of energy, causes piles, headache, biliousness, skin eruptions and a host of other disorders. For these disorders and for their root cause (constipation) Chas., Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness are unequalled. Mrs S Williams, of Lonhope, Gloucester, has proved this. " For fully twenty %ears " she.said to a " Dean Forest Mercury Reporter,"l was a martyr to constipa- tion. It brought on piles, and biliousness, robbed me of appetite and energy, and made my life a burden to me. Many a weary day have I spent weighed down with a feeling of tatter wretchedness and not caring whether I lived or died. Doctors ? Yes, I tried several but no amount of doctoring seemed capable of affording me the slightest relief ; and by degrees I grew weaker and wearier. I also Buffered periodically from erysipelas. A few months ago I saw some striking testimony in a Birmingham paper to the success of Chas. Farde'a Bile Beans in cases of piles and constipation. So I deter- mined to give them a fair trial. Soon after beginning the course I felt a change. I became brighter, and as I persevered, with the Beans the distressing symptoms front which I had suffered so long began to leave me.My appetite improved, and my bowels were corrected and strengthened. I ceased to be troubled with constipa• tin, and what late no -Monger made me bilious. The erysipelas, too, disappeared completely, and I have had no further trouble from that complaint. Now, after a thorough course of the Beans, I feel stronger, more vigorous, and in better health generally than I have felt for many years. Thanks alone to Chao. Fords's Bile Bans, life for me has been made once more worth living." Chas. Forde's Bile Beans for Biliousness are unequalled for constipation, piles, billow; attacks, and all disorders arising from impaired digevtion and defective bile flow. nervousness, dizziness, palpitation, pimples, skin erup- tions, liver and kidney ailments, backache, chest pains. aommia colds, chills, rheumatism and all female ail- ments. LECTURE AT EARLESTOWN. Insect Life.

ired I

The first of the fcourse of lectures promoted by the Newton-in-Mal' edeld Lectures Committee,was delivered in the Town Hall, Earlestown on Wednesday evening by Professor E Enock. F.L.S., F.E.Sc., on "Wonders and Romance of Insect Life." Mr W Collingwood pre- sided, and the Hall was crowde dwith an interested audience. The Chairman said this was the commence- ment of the sixth season's lectures. There were six to be delivered this season, two of which, as they would notice were upon Electricity, and he had been asked by the Secretary to draw their special attention to that, and to say that the Committee hoped many of them would follow up those lectures by a course of instruction which the Committee hoped would be inaugurated after- wards.

The Lecturer said, as they, knew, his lecture was en- titled "Wonders and romance of insect life." The wonders and romance in the life history of a single insect were so great and so many, it would be an im- possibility for him to speak of many insects. He might give them the names of two or three hundreds,theLatin names. It was the general rule with eatomologists to make large collections of insects,a number of specimens, that was the aim. Very few entomologists, however, knew much about the life history of the insects they had collected. If they did not know much about the life history of an insect, they frequently made it up. Some time ago a prize was offered in the United States for the best life history of a Hessian fly, which was the greatest pest in the United States. It was a very difficult thir,g, to find out all about the life history of an insect. Re (the lecturer) had worked out the life history of the British trap door spiclor, which took him nearly 20 years to complete, but be had not yet been able to discover the age of the female. The first insect which he would speak of was the blight, the green fly. They were everywhere, he did not know a single tree or shrub,but what they could find a greenfly upon,and th y increased at an immense rate. The family of a pair or green flies would ontweight the population of China. Some people called insects dirty nasty creature., but that was a gross libel, as in the course of his 50 years' experience he had never seen a dirty insect. An enemy of the green fly was the wasp fly, whoge eggs were laid on the leaves of the trees, and when they were hatched a maggot appeared of which there were 31 species in England. The lantern was used from this stage, and some excellent illustrations were thrown upon the screen, the lecturer dilating at some length upon each. The illustrations included the wasp, or hovering fly, " Lace wing fly" which was also a foe to the green fly, the " Black currant gall mite," the greatest pest we have in Great Britain. the 1. Saw f1y."... The devil's coach horse beetle," " The fairy fly," " The battle-door wing fly," dcc. The descriptions were most interesting, but the lecturer asserted that the American Government were at least 100 years ahead of us in their knowledge of insect life. A vote of thanks to the lecturer was moved by Dr Latham, and seconded by Mr G Goff, and heartily I accorded.

The lecturer said he was obliged for the vote of thanks. It was always a great privilege to him to speak of the wonderful works of the Creator. We knew so vlry little abut them, and life was not long enough to allow of a proper study being made. The common house fly's history had never yet been published. He knew something about it but he did not know it entirely. He conelLded by moving a vote of thanks to the chair- man, and he would like to give his very hearty thanks to the lanternimt (Mr Graham) for the manner in which be bad shown the slides. He had never had them shown better, and be wag a very particular man, but be must say he had never had them brought out so sharp as on that occasion. Both votes were duly carried.

Saturday's match at Warrington resulted unmistak- ably in victory for the better team. The Rangers out- played Warrington in every department, but more particularly in the scrimmages. Here they got the ball three times out of four and it was only the fine defence of the home backs that kept the score down. The Warrington forwards deserve, individually and collec- tively, kicking. They made a disgracefullyipoor show on Saturday, and seemed able neither to push in the pack nor run in the open. Once upon a tittle their following-up was a feature but now they seem totally unable to get under the ball place it where you will. The fault is as much with the committee as with the players. If they had only secured a couple of smart now forwards they would have held the whip-hand of the present eight who do not 'see any necessity to play for their places.' Last season Edmondson and Fell effected the desired reformation •, and Oram and Trotter I have done the same for Broughton whose forwards just now will want some beating. Warrington's efforts to find new blood this season have failed miserably, and whatever the difficulties may be absolute failure takes a lot of excusing. •

--- The officials are entitled to credit for one thing, and that is their smartness in getting the ground cleared of Saturday's fall of snow. By 3 p.m. the playing portion was absolutely free from the encumbrance and with the exception of a soft patch in the centre was in fair condition. The "going "was heavy, certainly, but it was the same for both sides, and only one side showed leg-weariness—that was the losing side. Everything considered the game was an excellent one, for the Rangers played bright, open football, and kept things on the move. Though W James and Hogg were absentees the difference was not very apparent, while Warrington sorely missed Isherwood and Allen. There was plenty of good back play and it was this which won the game, Wilson and Hampson getting both the Rangers tries. On the home side Fish had few oppor- tunities, which is usually the case when Isherwood is absent.

" Bob " Wilson was the best man on the field, and his dashing unselfish centre play excited general ad- miration. He is a well-built fellow and takes a lot of stopping when in his stride, as the home team found out when he ran through them and scored behind the posts. With Hogg and W James on the Rangers score would probably have been larger, for Hampson was only moderate on the right wing, while the other three- quarters, Harry and Jackson were poor. The" big'un" is nothing like the footballer he once was. S. James played a grand game at half back and was well sup- ported by Barratt, who has plenty of football in him yet, notwithstanding his 36 years. Fielding was very safe at full back,though he does not strike the observer as being brilliant.

-- On the home side. Hallam, Fish, Burgess and Morrison were the only men who played up to form, the others being moderate. Burgess did splendid work while he was on the field, and the same can be said of Fish when he filled the vacancy. It Bate is too slow at passing for class football, and never makes headway himself.

Broughton Rangers are undoubtedly a 'fine teani,with no weak places, which is the reason they are so well ahead of all competitors this season. There are other fine teams, but they have a soft spot in their armour. Oldham are rocky at three-quarter; Bradford at half; Swinton at full back ; Salford at three-quarter; and Warrington in a few places. The Rangers are strong all round, even with a man or two off, though there is room for improvement at three-quarter. The •left wing is never as good as the right, and another man of Wilson's class would make all the difference.

Swinton got a shock at flalifax on Saturday, and went down very unexpectedly. One wonders, but it is the smart and energetic forward play of the blue and whites which is doing the damage. Oldham went down before Salford, while Runcorn gave evidence of improvement by winning handsomely at Hunslet. Leigh walloped Hull, and the scribes of the colliery town are now screaming with delight. They have a reputation for screaming to keep up, and don't you forget it.

On Wednesday Halifax showed how rapidly they are on the improve by winning at Oldham, the score in their favour being 10 points to 3 points. These fre- quent defeats must make the folk at Watersheddings rather glum.

Warrington will certainly have another stiff task to-day, for Salford have been playing well lately and may be relied upon to try and add another to their rare victories on Warrington soil. .The forwards and half backs have been giving a particularly good account of themselves, and in this department may have the pull over Warrington. At three-quarter we ought to hold our own comfortably, and, anyhow, Warrington must win, or the position of last of the Lancashire clubs will certainly be ours.

Fell and Isherwood have both recovered from injured knees,but Allen is still under medical supervision with injured ribs.

-- The first of February has arrived, and with it the last chance of registering new players for the cupties dis- appears. Warrington will therefore have to depend entirely upon existing talent and the capability of that for taking the team far into the cup tourney is doubtful, especially if two or three of the men get string up. Somehow one cannot feel altogether satis- fied that all has been done that might have been done in the way of strengthening the team. I still think if it was possible to get a couple of forwards last season it is still possible, and they ought to have been had for love or money. Anyone could see how badly they were required last Saturday.

The Warrington officials are' naturally aggrieved by the inconsiderateness of that Broughtonians and an effort will be made to recover expenses,

To-day the "A " team will probably get a drubbing at Salford. but with the strong team selected they should make a fair show.
