LOCAL TEA MEETINGr' • - - - THE NEW VICAR OF ST PAUL'S WELCOMED. Additional interest was lent to the proceedings at the annual congregational tea party and entertainment in connection withSt Paul's Church, 'which was held in the Pare Hall on Monday evening, by the fact that the vicar-eleet (Rev W Bracecenip) was 'introduced to the congregation and delivered his first speech to his future parishioners. The event was attended with complete SUCCESS, about 450 members of the coagregation and friends sitting down to a capital tea. An enjoyable en. tertainment followed, Alderman Sutton occupying the chair.

rhe CiLSIRMAN, after referring to the great progress which had been made in the work ot the Church dur- ing the pest twenty cie thirty years, said the secret of the improvements was that during the period named they had bad three most energetic vicars, who had been indefatigable in their efforts to benefit and improve tee parish in every way. Although they had only had their present Vicar with them for three years he had already done much good in the parish, and had sueceeded in gaining the good esteem of every- one in Warrington. Of the many very good things which Mr Tarbuck had done for the parish one of the most useful and beneficial undertakings he had started was the provision of an endowment feed, which he (the Chairman) was sure they would all agree was very much needed. The living of St Paul's was very small and in- sufficient for the requirements of the Vicar,who, in such a populous parisb,was called upon to contribute so much out of his own pocket every year for charitable pur- poses. The fund 90 far had been very successful and he hoped it would continue to be so. Their Vicar and his good wife were about to leave them and they would all regret their departure very much. They would,however, console themselves with the fact that their less would be Altriochane's gain, When the time for the departure of Mr and Mrs Tarbuck came they would wish them " God-speed." and more happiness and prosperity in the future. (Applause). Mr. CERMET (people's warden) in alluding to the progress of the put year, said the amount received from pew rents was the largest since the church had teen re-seated. Referring to the forthcoming departure of Mr and Mrs Tarbuck, he said they had done a vast amount of work during the three years they had been in the parish, and he' was sure it was appreciated by the congregation. (Hear. hear.) They had to welcome the new Vicar and Mrs Bracecamp. (Applause.) He congratulated Mr Bracecamp upon his appointment. Mr Tarbuck was a hard man to follow, but if Mr Brace- camp continued the work which had beers started the congregation would back him up. (Applause.) Mr J. H. SNETHugST said he was delighted to know that a record had been established in the matter of the church collections this year, as up to Sunday the gross receipts came to £317 143 11d, compared with £309 17s ed the previous year. (Applause). The sum of £3OO had been raised for the endowment fund, and that had been met'by an equal grant of £3OO by the Ecclesias- tical Commissioners,with the result that the endowment had been raieed by £lB per annum. Continuing Mr Smethurst paid a tribute to the work of the late Mr William Chantry who succeeded him in the office of Vicar's warden. Referring to the approaching departure of the Vicar the speaker said they could not have Lad a better Vicar than Mr 'Fatback. Whilst regretting the departure ofs Dar Tarbuck they sincerely welcomed Mr Bracecamp to the parish. So far as he (the speaker) was concerned he should he quite willing to help their new Vicar as much as he could. They would no doubt have the ser- vices carried on in the same way and if Mr Bracecamp followed in the footsteps of his predecessor, St. Paul's would no doubt continue to fleariele. (Applause). The Rev. E. R. TARBUCX, who had a cordial recep- tion, said be thought when be was preaching to them on Sunday night whether be could ever live amongst such kindly hearted and enthusiastically loyal people again. It had been a great privilege to him, and it bad helped him very greatly in his spiritual life to be so loyally and readily supported in his work. He had every confidence that everyone of them, whether it be Vicar's warden or right through the list of officers to the smallest choir boy, would be as kiud to their new Vicar asithey had been to him. Mr. Bracecamp 'had for about 14 years been a professionel worker in the Church of England ; the work had been of a responsible and intricate character, and he was quite sure from his own observation that be would be a suitable successor at St Paul's. (Applause.) Mr Bracecamp did not desire that any change of any kind should be made, as for as he could possibly promise that at present, and carry it out in the future, either at the church, or in connection with the varied organizations of the parish . There would be no change except in the Vicar and, per- haps, in the improvement of various institutions already in existence. He hoped his work would be as happy as his (Mr. Tarbuck's) had been. The Rev. W figACECASIP, who had a splendid recep- tion said that little did be think when he Ws, working in the next perish that be would ever 135 asked to ap- pear in the position in which bet found himself that evening. When he and Mrs Bracecamp packed up a short time ago they thought they were saying " good- bye "to Warrington, but it was while be was on his way to York to be licensed to the curacy at Sheffield that he got the news that Mr Tarbuck was leavine his parish and that he was intormed that his name was before the congrega- tion of St Paul's. He felt very much upset, of course. and didn't know what to do, and when the offer of the living came he felt that it was a direct call from God. Working men, some working men at any rate, did' not like to hear a parson talking about a "a call from God," because they said that when a parson was " called " he got more money. But with him it was not a case of Ratting more money by coming to St. Paul's, but that he regarded it as a direct call from God, as he happened. to be in as good a position at Sheffield so far as money matters were con- cerned. However, having aceepted the living, he was prepared to do his duty, and no Eegliebrnen could do more. (Appleuee). Re knew he had 'a difficult man to follow, but if they would stand by him the work would be easy. United they would stand; divided they would fell- l&Pplauee). Therefore let them try to take Mr Tarbuck as their pattern, follow in his footsteps, and they would work together well and harmoniously. (Applause). A vote of thanks having been accorded to the Chair- man a most interesting and varied entertainment was submitted. Part songs were given in excellent style by the chcir and the members of St Faul's Ladies' Orchestra delighted the audience with a number of selections which were played in a highly creditable manner. A couple of humorous sketches were contributed by Mr Clifford Knowles, who scored a great success, being encore several times in succession. Mr F Scattergood sang "Genevieve" in such a pleasing manner as to gain a recall. A pretty Scotch dance was cleverly executed by eeveral pupils of Miss Sinks and the item so pleased the audience that a repetition was demanded. The con- cluding item of the programme was a laughable farce entitled" Captain Smith," the characters being sUS- tsined by the following :—Sir Charles Chatterton, Mr H B Houghton ; Captain Smith, Mr A H Burgess ; Fido, Mr It G S Finney ; Mrs Clapperclaw, Miss D Reid ; Mies Stella Smi`h, Miss Greenhough ; Arabella. Miss K Wain- wright ; Dolly, MiFS a Houghton. Mr JCoxon was an efficient stage manager. The artistes acquitted them- selves admirably and the performance was thoroughly enj4yed. On Tuesday evening a successful dance was held in the same building, the music being supplied by Mr Andertonre band.

GOOD TEMPLARS'% The Hope of Earlestown Lodge weekly raesting was held in the Co-operative Hall,,Wargrave-road, on Monday evenhig, Bro Cntler, CT, presiding, when two candidates were initiated and two re-admitted to member6hip. The,Lodge was closed in the usual manner by Bro Cutler, c T. 20,T11E PROPOED EXTENSION OF Sr. JOHN'S CHURCH, EARLESTOWN. A meeting of Churchmen was bald in the vestry of St John's Church, on Thursday evening for the purpose of taking into consideration the question of providing funds for the completion of St John's Church by the erection of a tower. The vicar (Rev T R Matthews) presided, and there were present Rev J C Matthews, Messrs T Molyneux, J Foden, Joseph Rylance, S Dalby, G Dale, Wild, Hackett Clough, Martindale, Boardman, Dean, Jones Pimblett, Mouncey, Monks and Findlay. The Chairman after explaining why they were ,brought together called upon Dr Mouncey to move a resolu- tion. Dr Mouncey saidithat'during the five years that had elapsed since the old committee last met several of the members had departed from the church, or, in other words were dead so far as church work was concerned, whilst others had been removed to higher spheres, lie therefore moved that taking these circumstances into account, a town's meeting should be held at a date to be fixed he thought about the last week in Lent would be the most convenient time, in a suitable place, pre- sumably the Town Hall. The reason he mentioned it as a town's meeting, was that he did not think it should be confined to St John's Church alone. No doubt there were several members of other churches in the district who would be willing to help them, if they were given an opportunity. He thought it would be a very good plan to ask all the influential men of the district to attend that, meeting, and they might ask Colonel Pilkington M.P. to take the chair. Mr Pimblett seconded the motion, which was carried unanimously. A small committee consisting of the Rev J C Matthews, Dr C Mouncey, Mr Boardman and Mr T Martindale were afterwards appointed to make all 'arrangements for the town's meeting and also to appoint the several officers necessary to carry the work on. The chairman stated in answer to a question that the amount in band in connection with the Church completion was about £154. The question of bazaars, sports, May Quesn &c, for increasing the funds was left in the bands of the General Meeting to be held.

EARLESTOWN DISTRICT SCHOOL. On Monday evening the junior scholars of the mixed department of the district; school gave an entertainment in the schoolroom before a crowded audience. The bill of fare consisted of a kinders- piel by the juniors,and miscellaneous items by the senior scholars. The kinderspiel was entitled "Queen Revel" and was given in a really excellent manner reflecting not only the greatest credit upon the teachers, and Mrs Brown headmistress and Mr Brown (master) but upon the children who proved themselves exceedingly apt pupils The characters represented included " Humpty Dumpty," " Red Riding flood " Wolf" " Fairies " " Mother Hubbard," "Imps" " Jack and Jill,"" Darby and Joan " "Rats Messenger,' Children in Games," and Queen Revel." The dresses of the youngsters were quite in accordance with the characters they repre- sented, andthey each sang or recited the old time nursery rhymes. Their representation was enthu elastically received and rapturously applauded The second pare comprised rui.cellaneous items, th chorus of 51 boys and girls singing the items put down to them in a capital manner, excellent time , and tune being kept. Sixteen boys trained by Mr ' Moss,who has recently returned from South Africa, 11 gave a clever exposition of military drill and they , performed the different evolutions like a machine ! Six boys sang the coon song " Niggers I Serenade,' in such a manner as to call forth a persisteut encore. They earned a similar compliment for the coon song Uncle Snow," and it is only fair to say that they richly deserved the compliment. An old English dance by eight boys and an equal numberof girls was so well done that a repetition was demanded. A. drill by six girls was excellently carried out. The solos " Way down the Swanee River," sung by one of the niggers, and the chorus, was swingingly taken up. During an interval the Rev T R Matthews spoke of the excellent manner in which the children had performed their various tasks. Such entertainments as these afforded an excellent training for the youngsters, who were taught to sing and to speak, and inspirel them with such an amuunt of confi- dence that in after years they would not be nervous when appearing before an audience. Not only so, but sucn entertainments were a fruitful source of income to a school where fees were notlin existence, and it was helpful in keeping the school going. Alluding to the necessary training, he said it was not work required of the teachers, but it was a duty they had taken up of their own accord. He ex• tended a public welcome to Mr Moss (assistant) who was a soldier returned from South Africa.—The entertainment was repeated on Tuesday evening and there was again a large audience.

THE EXAMINER. FRIDAY JANUARY 31, 1902 NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL. By " Ovalist." January has been a disastrous month for the War- rington club, as in that short space of time they have suffered defeat on their own ground on three occasions, while there is only a single victory away to compensate. The surprise win at Hull must have been a flash in the pan, for Warrington have gone down consistently ever since. Bradford put the stopper on Warrington's pre- tensions by wiping the floor with them on New Year's Day. That was a woeful start for the new year, and the omens of that day have so far been amply fulfilled. It is nothing but a disgrace for Warrington to lose on their own soil three times in one month. They will soon forfeit their reputation for being a formidable team to take down if they continue to drop points at Wilders- pool. Why, Salford must feel almost cocksure about to-day's match! Let us, for goodness sake; show the Reds that the Warrington team is not quite done with. To-day (Saturday) is February Ist. A turn in the tide is hoped for and expected!

In addition to losing points and matches in January Warrington have lost three men who may, be badly ' wanted when the cup ties commence. These three are Boardman, W Swift and Burgess, all dismissed from play by the referee on the ground of roughness or fight- ing. Burgess seems to me to be rather hardly dealt with. I did not see what his offence on Saturday was, but I hear that he used his feet when on ' the ground. and the ever-ready Barratt played "possum." Burgess himself is indignant at his fate,and sticks to it that he is innocent of offence. Anyhow it is likely 0 go hard with him when he comes before the Committee, for this is his second offence this season. And last time too he was not the cause of the trouble.

Tho afford committee have selected the following te stn.

D Smith, fullback; E A Bone, T Williams, J Party, and H Price, three-quarter backs ; D Davies and J Lomas, half-backs; P Tunney, J Rhapps, G Heath, H Buckler, J Williams, M Gldhill, W Brown, and R Shaw, forwards. H Shore and B Griffiths, reserves.

A Manchester correspondent says :--g, It will be no iced Griffiths has been displaced in favour of D Davies, who last week was played in Lomas's place owing to the latter being unable to obtain his permit, and it may be said he impressed the Salford officials so much with the capital game he played that they have felt justified in once more giving him a trial. The only other alteration to record is in the forwards, where J Williams, who has been absent from the field for several weeks, displaces Shore. A good deal of interest is centred in this encounter, as it is the first time this season these clubs have met, and although the Royal borough team are showing considerably improved form, they will have their work cut out to vanquish Warrington on their own ground, a feat any club are proud to accomplish." .

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

Continued from page G. RUNCORN CLUB.

NOTICIC TO QUIT. The Committee of Runcorn Football Club are now faced with a serious difficulty. Thai, have received notice to qnit the field, of which they have been tenants app roaching a quarter of a century. The owners,however give the club the alternative of purchasing the land. After the team won the Lancashire Championship in 1893-00, several hundreds of pounds were spent upon stands and field improvements, and at one time and another much monly has been sunk in drain- ing operations,Under these circumstances the notice is a very bitter pill. Fortunately the grand stand is built in sections and could easily be removed and re-erected on another site. Negotiations are proceeding with the solicitors to the owners on the question. and there is a hopeful feeling that the notic may te withdrawn. At the came time a keen look outi s • being kept for a suitable ground elsewhere. The expense of removal would be very heavy, and the finances of the club are In none too flourishing a condition at the present time.

NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

[BY A CORRESPONDENT] , FOOTBALL. Owing to Ilaydock withdrawing from the St. Helens Charity League, Earlestown were left With a blank date ou Saturday, and the same cause will have the 'same effect on next Saturday, unless a match is quickly fixed up. lhere was some.talk of playing the return League match with either Southport Central or Darwen,but this arrangement has evidently fallen through. It will be a great pity if the team is resting for two Saturdays in succession, especially as they art exhibiting such grand form at present. It would be a different thing altogether if they had been playing several mid-week matches and needed a rest but that is not the case, and the enforced idleness may have an adverse, instead of an invigorating effect upon the eleven.

Newton are still on the downward grade and the worst of the matter is that every week there are no half measures about their defeats. The score piled upon against them always assumes huge pro- portions. On Saturday they paid a visit to Wrex- ham—the prospective champions of the combination —and the Welshmen rubbed it in to the tune of seven goals to nil. It is impossible to account for the recent heavy defeats the Newtoners have suffered but it is practically certain that they will gain the wooden spoon of the combination, unless the turn ronnd in miraculous fashion and this hardly seems likely. WINWICE ASYLUM SEWAOIE QUESTION. The Cu= reported that the opinion of Mr Fitzgerald, 8.C., wick regard to the question of the disposal of the sewage from Winwick Aeylum had been obtairood. The other day he (Mr Sutton) met Mr Francomb (the chair- man of the Visiting Committee of Winwick Asylum) and had a conversation with hitnlon the matter. Ile told Mr Francomb that the Council had no desire whatever to litigate on the contrary they would be rleased to fled a way out of the difficulty without litigation, but unless the Council was treated in the same way that the dis- tricts of Whittingham and Rainhill were treated when new asylums were erected there they could not permit the thing to pass. Mr Sutton read correspoadence which had passed between himself and Mr Muspratt (clerk of the Asylums Visiting Coca- mittee) and-Messrs W D Jeans and Sons (who are acting on behalf of the Council) on the subject. Mr Loicormi said he would be glad if something was going to be done in the,matter. At present the smell was very had. _ _

Mr KNOWLES: Ibave beard you are likely to grow clover crops without manure. (Lauaiater.) On the proposition of Mr KNOWLES seconded b 7 Mr Nieman it was decided that the Chairman and Messrs Longton and Brooks should form a deputation to meet a committee of the Asylums Board to discuss the question. Mr Fonstraw now moved :—"That this Council con- sent to the terms proposed by the Corporation of War- rington for the extension of water mains in the Burton- wood parish, and that the Clerk A p ply to the Town Clerk for a draft contract thereon, and lay the same before the Council at their next meeting." He pointed out that the matter had been before the Council time after time. The Corporation in the first instance asked for a guarantee of si per cent. on the outlay, but ultimately consented to 5 per cent, and it was on these terms that he moved the contract be made. The Parish Council had unanimously agreed to the scheme. Mr JOHN WARBURTON addressed the meeting and said it would be very hard for the ratepayers to bear the expense of the extension. He presented a petition against the proposal signed by a number of ratepayers aaclfpointed out that there were at least 175 residents who did not require the water supply as they were already well provided for. •

In answer to Mr. FORSHAW the speaker said he was not prepared with an alternative scheme. At the invitation of the CHAIRMAN the Rev A M Mitchell expressed his views in the matter. He said he bad besought Mr. Fairclough to withdraw his opposition and fall into line. He was very sorry that Mr Fair- cl.4ugh and sevaral others had net done so. If some persons had a water supply it was quite evident that those who had not, could not be denied it with equal justice. He thought it was a great pity that the Parish Council had not appeared at that meeting in a body. He thought if they had done ro there would have been a mutual solution of the difficulty. Be knew from his own exf perience that during the past year people had been in extremes for water during the summer months and be did not think they ought to minimise the difficulty which undoubtedly existed. He trusted the Council would respond to the appeal of the Parish Council and agree with the arrangement. Ho understood that the Parish Council was uuani nous in voting for the sunray and if so he did not understand the position of Mr Fur. dough because he was a member of the Council. The parishioners had on two distinct occasions voted in favour of this supply and this showed that the Parish Council had the support of the parishioners generally. Mr KNOWLES seconded Mr Farshaw's proposition. and. Mr Lowarru,i supported, remarking that he knew what it waste be without water.

GROSS CRUELTY TO A DOG.

===== 0002601_19020131_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
Case at Newton Sessions.

At Newton Petty Sesaions, on Thursday, Frederick Lentz was charged by the Royal Society for the Preven- tion of Cruelty to Animals with cruelty to a dog. Mr Steel prosecuted, and Mr J Graham,of Wigan, defended Mr Steel said that James Rigby, of Penny-lane, Hay- dock, allowed his black retriever dog to go out for a run on the flad inst. It did not return until the next afternoon, when it crawled home in a most deplorable condition, Its left eye was hanging out on the cheek by the skin, two teeth were also hanging out, and the left foreleg was broken. Rigby attended to it. but on the Sunday, seeing that there was no chance of it recovering, he drowned lt.on a subsequent date he went to defendant's Irouse about the dog, and defendant admitted to him that he had thrashed the dog with a stick until the stick was broken, and then with a bar of iron. The dog was fastened up in his yard, and about ten o'clock he went to get some coal, when it flew out at him. He did not know how it came to be fastened up in his yard. Evidence was given by Rigby, Inspector Print, of the Royal Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals, Sergeant Walker, Constable Johnson, and two °there, bearing out this statement.

For the defence, it was urged that the dog flaw at Lentz and bit him, and be struck it in self-defence. Be denied that when the dog left his yard it eye was hanging out and that its teeth were also hanging and its leg broken. It was in the same condition as it was when it attacked him.

The magistrates retired, and on returning into court the Chairman said they had carefully considered the case,and were convinced that there had been very great cruelty used.—The defendant would be fined £1 and costs.

THE "CHURCH TIMES" AND THE REV JOHN YONGE. The " Church Times" of Friday's date contains the followieg The Rev • John Yonge, an Ind3pendant preacher in Warrington,bas pontifically informed two of the Dissent- ing sects that they are "the only true High Church- men." Whilst intending merely to strike a telling blow at " the Anglican," he has been 60 unlucky as to give a , sinister slap in the lace to the Presbyterians, the Uni- tarians, the Methodists, the Sweden hra.gians, and all the I other varieties of " Free Churches." It is Congrega- tionalist separation pure and simple, he contends, and nothing else, which has any right to call itself " High Church." All the parish churches, and all the Presby- terian and Wesleyan meeting-houses in the North are " Low Church." All the Independent and Baptist meeting-houses are" High Church." The reason why this denomination of " High Church " ought to be taken away from the Sacerdotalists and transferred to the Independent and Baptist sects, is no less intolerant than it is certainly impettinent. " The Baptists and Congregationalists are the only true High Churchmen," says this amazing dogmatist, " because with them the Ecclesia, the Assembly cf Believers, is supreme." This is to imply either that there are no " Believers" in the English Church, and no " Believers" in any of the "Free Churches " which are not Anabaptist or Con- gregationalist ; or else,that if there be any "Believers " accidently belonging to the parish church or to a Wes- leyan or Presbyterian chapel, they can only be jointed into the one, true, visible " Assembly of the Faithful " by turning Anabaptists or Independents. So rapid is " the decay ofiNonconformity " that we shall doubtless be told, witkin a few months, that the anti-sacerdota- lists are " the only true Sacerdotalists." We are not surprised that the " Christian World " should begin its new series of papers on" The Nonconformist Stand- point " with the frank confession " One of the most curious ironies of history is furnished by the contrast between the founders of Nonconformity and their modern descendants." 1 A Child's Singular Death. An inquest was conducted at the Police Station on Tuesday afternoon touching the death of Alice Smith, aged two years and nine months, the daughter of Peter Smith, a general labourer, resid- ing at 12, Margaret-street.—Deceased's mother informed the jury that at 9 30 un the evening of January let she undressed the deceased and placed her in a rocking chair whilst she attended to a younger child. Deceased fell off the chair on to the jockey bar which was on the floor. Seeing that the child's head was injured witness took bee to the Infirmary whore she died on Sun- day last from a frac uted skull.—A verdict of "Acci- dental death " was returned. a, \ = • .1- •I 1 -00 " • - - , ARCM STOCK OF MANDOLINES, BANJOES. _IJ GUITARS, VIOLINS, Ac. which took place. This was the second time during the eight years he had been a member of the Council that his business house had been mentioned. He could safely say that there was no member of the Council who could say he had transacted any of the Council's Lusi- nese on his premises. Mr KNOWLES said be had no intention to offend Mr Halton. Mr HALTON said they had had quite sufficient. Mr KNOWLES ; Nonsense. Mr FoissuAw said they must attend te business before pleasure and he put his resolution to the meeting. On a vote being taken Mr Forshaw's proposition was carried. HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. Dr SEPHTON reported that during the manth there bad been 26 births and 13 deaths, the death rate being equal to 12 per 1,000 against 10 far the corresponding period of last year. An outbreak of measles had occurred at Buttonwood and application was male for the schools to be closed. As 70 per cent, of the cnildren were absentees it would be desirable to close the schools. This course was agreed to. This was all the business of interest.
